, is described by the toroidal and poloidal angles. Figure adapted from
[
121
]
.

We will consider a volume,
V
, bounded by a toroidal surface,
S
. Suppose that we use a set of continuously nested toroidal surfaces,
\Gamma(r)
, as a radial coordinate
r
, such that the position within this volume can be expressed as
\textbf{x}(r,\theta,\phi)
. A vector field,
A
can be expressed in the basis of the gradients of the coordinates,

\displaystyle\textbf{A}=A_{r}\nabla r+A_{\theta}\nabla\theta+A_{\phi}\nabla\phi,

(A.1)

the covariant form, or the derivatives of the position vectors with respect to the coordinates,

\displaystyle\textbf{A}=A^{r}\frac{\partial\textbf{x}}{\partial r}+A^{\theta}\frac{\partial\textbf{x}}{\partial\theta}+A^{\phi}\frac{\partial\textbf{x}}{\partial\phi},

(A.2)

the contravariant form. The two basis vectors can be related through the dual relations,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\textbf{x}}{\partial x_{i}}=\frac{\nabla x_{j}\times\nabla x_{k}}{\nabla x_{i}\cdot\nabla x_{j}\times\nabla x_{k}},

(A.3)

where
(x_{i},x_{j},x_{k})=(r,\theta,\phi)
or cyclic permutations. Such a coordinate system is generally non-orthogonal, so
\partial\textbf{x}/\partial x_{i}
is not necessarily parallel to
\nabla x_{i}
. Several useful relations in non-orthogonal coordinate systems are summarized in Table
A.1
. For a more detailed discussion, refer to Chapter 2 in
[
54
]
.

Jacobian

\sqrt{g}=\left(\frac{\partial\textbf{x}}{\partial x_{i}}\times\frac{\partial\textbf{x}}{\partial x_{j}}\right)\cdot\frac{\partial\textbf{x}}{\partial x_{k}}=\left(\left(\nabla x_{i}\times\nabla x_{j}\right)\cdot\nabla x_{k}\right)^{-1}

Differential volume

d^{3}x=|\sqrt{g}|dx_{i}dx_{j}dx_{k}

Differential length

d\textbf{x}=\sum_{i=1}^{3}\frac{\partial\textbf{x}}{\partial x_{i}}dx_{i}

Differential surface area (constant
x_{k}
)

d^{2}x=|\sqrt{g}||\nabla x_{k}|dx_{i}dx_{j}

Divergence of vector field

\nabla\cdot\textbf{A}=\sum_{i=1}^{3}\frac{1}{\sqrt{g}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}\left(\sqrt{g}A^{i}\right)

Curl of vector field

\nabla\times\textbf{A}=\sum_{k=1}^{3}\frac{1}{\sqrt{g}}\left(\frac{\partial A_{j}}{\partial x_{i}}-\frac{\partial A_{i}}{\partial x_{j}}\right)\frac{\partial\textbf{x}}{\partial x_{k}}

Gradient of scalar

\nabla q=\sum_{i=1}^{3}\frac{\partial q}{\partial x_{i}}\nabla x_{i}

Table A.1
:
Summary of formulas used to describe the geometry of a non-orthogonal coordinate system
(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
. In the above,
\{i,j,k\}
is a cyclic permutation of
\{1,2,3\}
. Table adapted from
[
121
]
.

A.2
Flux coordinates

If magnetic surfaces exist, indicating that the magnetic field is tangent to a set of continuously nested toroidal surfaces, we can use the toroidal flux through such surfaces as a coordinate, defined as,

\displaystyle 2\pi\psi\equiv\int_{\mathcal{S}_{T}(\psi)}d^{2}x\,\textbf{B}\cdot\hat{\textbf{n}}.

(A.4)

In the above expression,
\mathcal{S}_{T}(\psi)
is an open surface such that
\partial\mathcal{S}_{T}(\psi)
is a loop on
\Gamma(\psi)
that closes after one poloidal rotation (Figure
A.2
). The unit normal is
\hat{\textbf{n}}
, often chosen to point in the direction of increasing
\phi
.

Figure A.2
:
The plasma domain,
V_{P}
, is bounded by a toroidal surface,
S_{P}
. We make the assumption that there exists a set of toroidal magnetic surfaces,
\Gamma(\psi)
. The toroidal flux through each of these surfaces is defined by (
A.4
) with
\mathcal{S}_{T}(\psi)
an open surface bounded by a poloidally closed curve on
\Gamma(\psi)
,
\partial\mathcal{S}_{T}(\psi)
.

Another choice for labeling magnetic surfaces is the poloidal flux function,
\chi
,

\displaystyle 2\pi\chi\equiv\int_{\mathcal{S}_{P}(\psi)}d^{2}x\,\textbf{B}\cdot\hat{\textbf{n}},

(A.5)

where
\mathcal{S}_{P}(\psi)
is an open surface such that
\partial\mathcal{S}_{P}(\psi)
is a loop on
\Gamma(\psi)
that closes after one toroidal rotation (Figure
A.3
).

Figure A.3
:
The poloidal flux through the magnetic surface,
\Gamma(\psi)
, is defined by (
A.5
) with
\mathcal{S}_{P}(\psi)
an open surface bounded by a toroidally closed curve on
\Gamma(\psi)
,
\partial\mathcal{S}_{P}(\psi)
.

The rotational transform quantifies the number of poloidal turns of a field line per toroidal turn,

\displaystyle\iota\equiv\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\sum_{k=1}^{n}\left(\Delta\theta\right)_{k}}{2\pi n}.

(A.6)

Here
(\Delta\theta)_{k}
is the change in poloidal angle in toroidal rotation
k
and
n
counts the toroidal turns. If flux surfaces exist, then the rotational transform can be computed from the derivative of the poloidal flux with respect to the toroidal flux,

\displaystyle\iota(\psi)=\chi^{\prime}(\psi),

(A.7)

If a flux label,
\psi
, is used as one of the coordinates, known as a flux coordinate system, then the contravariant form for the magnetic field simplifies,

\displaystyle\textbf{B}=B^{\theta}\frac{\partial\textbf{x}}{\partial\theta}+B^{\phi}\frac{\partial\textbf{x}}{\partial\phi},

(A.8)

from the assumption that
\textbf{B}\cdot\nabla\psi=0
. Given
\nabla\cdot\textbf{B}=0
and using (
A.3
), we can express the magnetic field as,

\displaystyle\textbf{B}=\nabla\psi\times\nabla\left(\theta-\iota(\psi)\phi+\lambda(\psi,\theta,\phi)\right),

(A.9)

where
\lambda(\psi,\theta,\phi)
is
2\pi
-periodic in
\theta
and
\phi
(Section 11.1 in
[
121
]
).

In a flux-coordinate system, the flux-surface average,

\displaystyle\langle A\rangle_{\psi}=\frac{\int_{0}^{2\pi}d\theta\int_{0}^{2\pi}d\phi\,\sqrt{g}A}{V^{\prime}(\psi)},

(A.10)

appears in many calculations, where

\displaystyle V^{\prime}(\psi)=\int_{0}^{2\pi}d\theta\int_{0}^{2\pi}d\phi\,\sqrt{g},

(A.11)

is the differential volume associated with a change in flux. The flux-surface average can be equivalently defined as the average over the infinitesimal volume between flux surfaces,

\displaystyle\left\langle A\right\rangle_{\psi}=\lim_{\Delta V\rightarrow 0}\frac{1}{\Delta V}\left(\int_{V_{P}(\psi)+\Delta V}d^{3}x\,A-\int_{V_{P}(\psi)}d^{3}x\,A\right),

(A.12)

where
V_{P}(\psi)
is the volume enclosed by a surface labeled by
\psi
and
V_{P}(\psi)+\Delta V
is the volume of a neighboring surface. The flux-surface average is discussed in more detail in Section 4.9 of
[
54
]
.

A.3
Magnetic coordinates

A flux coordinate system can be defined with many choices of poloidal and toroidal angles. With some choices of these angles, the contravariant expression for the magnetic field can simplify further. Given (
A.9
), the definition of the poloidal and toroidal angles can be shifted to
\vartheta
and
\varphi
such that the magnetic field can be expressed as,

\displaystyle\textbf{B}=\nabla\psi\times\nabla\left(\vartheta-\iota(\psi)\varphi\right).

(A.13)

Such angles define a magnetic coordinate system. For example, one choice is
\vartheta=\theta+\lambda(\psi,\theta,\phi)
and
\varphi=\phi
. For any choice of
\varphi
, there is a corresponding choice of
\vartheta
that defines a magnetic coordinate system. With this choice of angles, the magnetic field lines are said to be straight in the
\vartheta-\varphi
plane,

\displaystyle\frac{d\vartheta(l)}{d\varphi(l)}=\frac{\textbf{B}\cdot\nabla\vartheta}{\textbf{B}\cdot\nabla\varphi}=\iota(\psi),

(A.14)

with a slope given by the rotational transform. Here
l
measures length along a field line such that
df/dl=\hat{\textbf{b}}\cdot\nabla f
for any quantity
f
, where
\hat{\textbf{b}}=\textbf{B}/B
is the unit vector in the direction of the magnetic field.

From the covariant form for the magnetic field,

\displaystyle\textbf{B}=B_{\vartheta}\nabla\vartheta+B_{\varphi}\nabla\varphi+B_{\psi}\nabla\psi,

(A.15)

we can compute the net toroidal and poloidal currents enclosed by the surface labeled by
\psi
,

\displaystyle I_{T}(\psi)

\displaystyle\equiv\int_{\mathcal{S}_{T}(\psi)}d^{2}x\,\textbf{J}\cdot\hat{\textbf{n}}=\frac{1}{\mu_{0}}\oint_{\partial\mathcal{S}_{T}(\psi)}d\textbf{l}\cdot\textbf{B}=\frac{1}{\mu_{0}}\int_{0}^{2\pi}d\vartheta\,B_{\vartheta}

(A.16a)

\displaystyle I_{P}(\psi)

\displaystyle\equiv\int_{\mathcal{S}_{P}(\psi)}d^{2}x\,\textbf{J}\cdot\hat{\textbf{n}}=\frac{1}{\mu_{0}}\oint_{\partial\mathcal{S}_{P}(\psi)}d\textbf{l}\cdot\textbf{B}=\frac{1}{\mu_{0}}\int_{0}^{2\pi}d\varphi\,B_{\varphi},

(A.16b)

where
\mathcal{S}_{T}
is defined in Figure
A.2
and
\mathcal{S}_{P}
is defined in Figure
A.3
. Under the additional assumption that
\textbf{J}\cdot\nabla\psi=0
, which follows from MHD force balance (
1.3a
) with
p(\psi)
, we can write the covariant form as,

\displaystyle\textbf{B}=I(\psi)\nabla\vartheta+G(\psi)\nabla\varphi+K(\psi,\vartheta,\varphi)\nabla\psi+\nabla H(\psi,\vartheta,\varphi),

(A.17)

where
I(\psi)=\mu_{0}I_{T}(\psi)/(2\pi)
and
G(\psi)=\mu_{0}I_{P}(\psi)/(2\pi)
. See Section 2.5 in
[
97
]
, Section 9.2 in
[
121
]
, and Chapter 6.5 of
[
54
]
for details.

A.4
Boozer coordinates

As previously mentioned, there are many choices of magnetic coordinates corresponding to different choices of toroidal angle,
\varphi
. Suppose we begin with a system defined by
(\psi,\vartheta,\varphi)
and want to transform for a system defined by
(\psi,\vartheta^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime})
. In order for the primed system to remain a magnetic coordinate system, we must have
\varphi^{\prime}=\varphi+\gamma(\psi,\vartheta,\varphi)
and
\vartheta^{\prime}=\vartheta+\iota(\psi)\gamma(\psi,\vartheta,\varphi)
, where
\gamma(\psi,\vartheta,\varphi)
is
2\pi
-periodic in
\vartheta
and
\varphi
. To construct the Boozer coordinate system
[
23
]
, we will make a particular choice for
\gamma
to simplify the covariant form for the magnetic field (
A.17
). The corresponding changes to the quantities appearing in the covariant form (
A.17
) are

\displaystyle H^{\prime}

\displaystyle=H-\left(\iota(\psi)I(\psi)+G(\psi)\right)\gamma(\psi,\vartheta,\varphi)

(A.18a)

\displaystyle K^{\prime}

\displaystyle=K+\gamma(\psi,\vartheta,\varphi)\left(\iota(\psi)I^{\prime}(\psi)+G^{\prime}(\psi)\right).

(A.18b)

Boozer coordinates are defined such that
H^{\prime}=0
, or
\gamma(\psi,\vartheta,\varphi)=H(\psi,\vartheta,\varphi)/(\iota(\psi)I(\psi)+G(\psi))
. With this choice of transformation, we will denote
\vartheta_{B}=\vartheta+\iota\gamma
and
\varphi_{B}=\varphi+\gamma
. The covariant form becomes,

\displaystyle\textbf{B}=I(\psi)\nabla\vartheta_{B}+G(\psi)\nabla\varphi_{B}+K(\psi,\vartheta_{B},\varphi_{B})\nabla\psi.

(A.19)

By dotting the covariant with the contravariant form, we obtain an expression for the Jacobian,

\displaystyle\sqrt{g}=\frac{1}{\nabla\psi\times\nabla\vartheta_{B}\cdot\nabla\varphi_{B}}=\frac{G(\psi)+\iota(\psi)I(\psi)}{B^{2}}.

(A.20)

We note that the Jacobian only varies on a surface through the magnetic field strength; thus each of the contravariant and covariant components of the magnetic field, except for
K(\psi,\vartheta_{B},\varphi_{B})
, possesses the same property. (The radial covariant component,
K(\psi,\vartheta_{B},\varphi_{B})
, is related to the field strength through the MHD force balance equation (
1.3a
).) For this reason, the Boozer coordinate system is extremely convenient for analyzing guiding center motion and neoclassical transport, as we will in Chapter
4
.

Appendix B:   Justification for current potential

In this Appendix, we justify the form for a continuous current density supported on a toroidal surface,
S_{C}
,

\displaystyle\textbf{J}_{C}(\theta,\phi)=\hat{\textbf{n}}\times\nabla\Phi,

(B.1)

where
\hat{\textbf{n}}
is the unit normal vector.

We consider an extension of
\textbf{J}_{C}
in a neighborhood of
S_{C}
of width
\Delta b
,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\textbf{J}}_{C}(b,\widetilde{\theta},\widetilde{\phi})=\textbf{J}_{C}(\theta,\phi),

(B.2)

where we define extensions of
\theta
and
\phi
as,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\theta}(\textbf{x})

\displaystyle=\theta(\textbf{x}-b(\textbf{x})\nabla b)

(B.3a)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\phi}(\textbf{x})

\displaystyle=\phi(\textbf{x}-b(\textbf{x})\nabla b),

(B.3b)

or a normal projection onto
S_{C}
. We consider
b\in[-\frac{\Delta b}{2},\frac{\Delta b}{2}]
to be a “thickened” region of continuous current density. We impose the constraint that
\nabla\cdot\widetilde{\textbf{J}}_{C}=0
, expressed in the
(b,\widetilde{\theta},\widetilde{\phi})
coordinate system (Table
A.1
),

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{g}}\left(\frac{\partial\left(\sqrt{g}\widetilde{\textbf{J}}_{C}\cdot\nabla b\right)}{\partial b}+\frac{\partial\left(\sqrt{g}\widetilde{\textbf{J}}_{C}\cdot\nabla\widetilde{\theta}\right)}{\partial\widetilde{\theta}}+\frac{\partial\left(\sqrt{g}\widetilde{\textbf{J}}_{C}\cdot\nabla\widetilde{\phi}\right)}{\partial\widetilde{\phi}}\right)=0,

(B.4)

where
\sqrt{g}=\partial\textbf{x}/\partial b\cdot\left(\partial\textbf{x}/\partial\widetilde{\theta}\times\partial\textbf{x}/\partial\widetilde{\phi}\right)

By the definition of our extension, the first term will vanish. In the limit that
\Delta b\rightarrow 0
, the divergence-free condition is expressed as,

\displaystyle\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\textbf{J}_{C}\equiv\frac{1}{\sqrt{g}}\left(\frac{\partial\left(\sqrt{g}J^{\theta}\right)}{\partial\theta}+\frac{\partial\left(\sqrt{g}J^{\phi}\right)}{\partial\phi}\right)=0,

(B.5)

where we have expressed the current in the contravariant basis as
\textbf{J}_{C}=J^{\theta}\partial\textbf{x}/\partial\theta+J^{\phi}\partial\textbf{x}/\partial\phi
and
\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot
is the
surface divergence
(Appendix 3 in
[
229
]
). For a continuous current density, Ampere’s law (
1.3b
) implies that
\nabla\cdot\textbf{J}=0
. Thus the equivalent condition for a current supported on a surface is
\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\textbf{J}_{C}=0

[
11
]
. The surface divergence of a vector field tangent to a surface
\Gamma
(
\textbf{A}\cdot\hat{\textbf{n}}=0
on
\Gamma
) defined in terms of a general continuous extension,
\widetilde{\textbf{A}}
in a neighborhood of
\Gamma
is,

\displaystyle\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\textbf{A}\equiv\left(\nabla\cdot\widetilde{\textbf{A}}\right)\big\rvert_{\Gamma}-\hat{\textbf{n}}\cdot\left(\nabla\widetilde{\textbf{A}}\right)\big\rvert_{\Gamma}\cdot\hat{\textbf{n}}.

(B.6)

In (
B.2
), we have defined our extension such that
\nabla b\cdot\left(\nabla\widetilde{\textbf{J}}_{C}\right)=0
such that the second term in the above expression vanishes.

Given (
B.5
), we can write,

\displaystyle J^{\theta}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{g}}\frac{\partial\Phi(\theta,\phi)}{\partial\phi}

(B.7a)

\displaystyle J^{\phi}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{g}}\frac{\partial\Phi(\theta,\phi)}{\partial\theta},

(B.7b)

where,

\displaystyle\Phi=\int d\theta\,\sqrt{g}J^{\phi}.

(B.7c)

In other words,

\displaystyle\textbf{J}_{C}=\hat{\textbf{n}}\times\nabla\Phi.

(B.8)

Appendix C:   Adjoint derivative at fixed
J_{\text{max}}

We enforce
J_{\text{max}}=
constant in the REGCOIL solve in order to obtain the regularization parameter
\lambda
by requiring that the following constraint be satisfied within a given tolerance,

\displaystyle G\left(\Omega,\overrightarrow{\bm{\Phi}}(\Omega,\lambda)\right)=J_{\text{max}}\left(\Omega,\overrightarrow{\bm{\Phi}}(\Omega,\lambda)\right)-J^{\text{target}}_{\text{max}}=0.

(C.1)

Here
J^{\text{target}}_{\text{max}}
is the target maximum current density and
\overrightarrow{\bm{\Phi}}
is chosen to satisfy the forward equation (
3.8
),

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{\textbf{F}}\left(\Omega,\overrightarrow{\bm{\Phi}},\lambda\right)=\overleftrightarrow{\textbf{A}}(\Omega,\lambda)\overrightarrow{\bm{\Phi}}-\overrightarrow{\textbf{b}}(\Omega,\lambda)=0.

(C.2)

A log-sum-exponent function is used to approximate the maximum function, similar to that used to approximate
d_{\text{coil-plasma}}
(
3.24
),

\displaystyle J_{\text{max}}\approx J_{\text{max},\,\text{lse}}=\frac{1}{p}\log\left(\frac{\int_{S_{C}}d^{2}x\,\exp\left(pJ\right)}{A_{\text{coil}}}\right).

(C.3)

We compute the total differential of
\overrightarrow{\textbf{F}}
,

d\overrightarrow{\textbf{F}}(\Omega,\overrightarrow{\bm{\Phi}},\lambda)=\sum_{m,n}\left(\frac{\partial\overleftrightarrow{\textbf{A}}(\Omega,\lambda)}{\partial\Omega_{m,n}}\overrightarrow{\bm{\Phi}}-\frac{\partial\overrightarrow{\textbf{b}}(\Omega,\lambda)}{\partial\Omega_{m,n}}\right)d\Omega_{m,n}+\overleftrightarrow{\textbf{A}}d\overrightarrow{\bm{\Phi}}\\
+\left(\overleftrightarrow{\textbf{A}}^{K}\overrightarrow{\bm{\Phi}}-\overrightarrow{\textbf{b}}^{K}\right)d\lambda=0.

(C.4)

Here
\overleftrightarrow{\textbf{A}}^{K}=\partial\overleftrightarrow{\textbf{A}}/\partial\lambda
and
\overrightarrow{\textbf{b}}^{K}=\partial\overrightarrow{\textbf{b}}/\partial\lambda
. We left multiply by
\overleftrightarrow{\textbf{A}}^{-1}
and solve for
d\overrightarrow{\bm{\Phi}}
such that
d\overrightarrow{\textbf{F}}(\Omega,\overrightarrow{\bm{\Phi}},\lambda)=0
,

\displaystyle d\overrightarrow{\bm{\Phi}}=-\sum_{m,n}\overleftrightarrow{\textbf{A}}^{-1}\left(\frac{\partial\overleftrightarrow{\textbf{A}}(\Omega,\lambda)}{\partial\Omega_{m,n}}\overrightarrow{\bm{\Phi}}-\frac{\partial\overrightarrow{\textbf{b}}(\Omega,\lambda)}{\partial\Omega_{m,n}}\right)d\Omega_{m,n}-\overleftrightarrow{\textbf{A}}^{-1}\left(\overleftrightarrow{\textbf{A}}^{K}\overrightarrow{\bm{\Phi}}-\overrightarrow{\textbf{b}}^{K}\right)d\lambda.

(C.5)

We also compute the total differential of
G
,

\displaystyle dG(\Omega,\overrightarrow{\bm{\Phi}})=\sum_{m,n}\frac{\partial G(\Omega,\overrightarrow{\bm{\Phi}})}{\partial\Omega_{m,n}}d\Omega_{m,n}+\frac{\partial G(\Omega,\overrightarrow{\bm{\Phi}})}{\partial\overrightarrow{\bm{\Phi}}}\cdot d\overrightarrow{\bm{\Phi}}=0.

(C.6)

Using the form for
d\overrightarrow{\bm{\Phi}}
(
C.5
), we compute
d\lambda
in terms of
d\Omega_{m,n}
,

d\lambda=\left(\frac{\partial G(\Omega,\overrightarrow{\bm{\Phi}})}{\partial\overrightarrow{\bm{\Phi}}}\cdot\left[\overleftrightarrow{\textbf{A}}^{-1}\left(\overleftrightarrow{\textbf{A}}^{K}\overrightarrow{\bm{\Phi}}-\overrightarrow{\textbf{b}}^{K}\right)\right]\right)^{-1}\\
\times\sum_{m,n}\left(\frac{\partial G(\Omega,\overrightarrow{\bm{\Phi}})}{\partial\Omega_{m,n}}-\frac{\partial G(\Omega,\overrightarrow{\bm{\Phi}})}{\partial\overrightarrow{\bm{\Phi}}}\cdot\left[\overleftrightarrow{\textbf{A}}^{-1}\left(\frac{\partial\overleftrightarrow{\textbf{A}}(\Omega,\lambda)}{\partial\Omega_{m,n}}\overrightarrow{\bm{\Phi}}-\frac{\partial\overrightarrow{\textbf{b}}(\Omega,\lambda)}{\partial\Omega_{m,n}}\right)\right]\right)d\Omega_{m,n}.

(C.7)

Using (
C.5
) and (
C.7
), the derivative of
\overrightarrow{\bm{\Phi}}
with respect to
\Omega_{m,n}
subject to equations (
C.1
) and (
C.2
) is given by the following expression,

\frac{\partial\overrightarrow{\bm{\Phi}}(\Omega,\lambda(\Omega))}{\partial\Omega_{m,n}}=-\overleftrightarrow{\textbf{A}}^{-1}\left(\frac{\partial\overleftrightarrow{\textbf{A}}(\Omega,\lambda)}{\partial\Omega_{m,n}}\overrightarrow{\bm{\Phi}}-\frac{\partial\overrightarrow{\textbf{b}}(\Omega,\lambda)}{\partial\Omega_{m,n}}\right)-\frac{\overleftrightarrow{\textbf{A}}^{-1}\left(\overleftrightarrow{\textbf{A}}^{K}\overrightarrow{\bm{\Phi}}-\overrightarrow{\textbf{b}}^{K}\right)}{\frac{\partial G(\Omega,\overrightarrow{\bm{\Phi}})}{\partial\overrightarrow{\bm{\Phi}}}\cdot\left[\overleftrightarrow{\textbf{A}}^{-1}\left(\overleftrightarrow{\textbf{A}}^{K}\overrightarrow{\bm{\Phi}}-\overrightarrow{\textbf{b}}^{K}\right)\right]}\\
\times\left(\frac{\partial G(\Omega,\overrightarrow{\bm{\Phi}})}{\partial\Omega_{m,n}}-\frac{\partial G(\Omega,\overrightarrow{\bm{\Phi}})}{\partial\overrightarrow{\bm{\Phi}}}\cdot\left[\overleftrightarrow{\textbf{A}}^{-1}\left(\frac{\partial\overleftrightarrow{\textbf{A}}(\Omega,\lambda)}{\partial\Omega_{m,n}}\overrightarrow{\bm{\Phi}}-\frac{\partial\overrightarrow{\textbf{b}}(\Omega,\lambda)}{\partial\Omega_{m,n}}\right)\right]\right).

(C.8)

Here
\overrightarrow{\bm{\Phi}}
is understood to be a function of
\Omega
and
\lambda
through (
C.2
) and
\lambda
is understood to be a function of
\Omega
through (
C.1
). We use the adjoint method to avoid solving a linear system involving the operator
\overleftrightarrow{\textbf{A}}
for each
\Omega_{m,n}
,

\frac{\partial\overrightarrow{\bm{\Phi}}(\Omega,\lambda(\Omega))}{\partial\Omega_{m,n}}=-\overleftrightarrow{\textbf{A}}^{-1}\left(\frac{\partial\overleftrightarrow{\textbf{A}}(\Omega,\lambda)}{\partial\Omega_{m,n}}\overrightarrow{\bm{\Phi}}-\frac{\partial\overrightarrow{\textbf{b}}(\Omega,\lambda)}{\partial\Omega_{m,n}}\right)-\frac{\overleftrightarrow{\textbf{A}}^{-1}\left(\overleftrightarrow{\textbf{A}}^{K}\overrightarrow{\bm{\Phi}}-\overrightarrow{\textbf{b}}^{K}\right)}{\frac{\partial G(\Omega,\overrightarrow{\bm{\Phi}})}{\partial\overrightarrow{\bm{\Phi}}}\cdot\left[\overleftrightarrow{\textbf{A}}^{-1}\left(\overleftrightarrow{\textbf{A}}^{K}\overrightarrow{\bm{\Phi}}-\overrightarrow{\textbf{b}}^{K}\right)\right]}\\
\times\left(\frac{\partial G(\Omega,\overrightarrow{\bm{\Phi}})}{\partial\Omega_{m,n}}-\left[\left(\overleftrightarrow{\textbf{A}}^{T}\right)^{-1}\frac{\partial G(\Omega,\overrightarrow{\bm{\Phi}})}{\partial\overrightarrow{\bm{\Phi}}}\right]\cdot\left(\frac{\partial\overleftrightarrow{\textbf{A}}(\Omega,\lambda)}{\partial\Omega_{m,n}}\overrightarrow{\bm{\Phi}}-\frac{\partial\overrightarrow{\textbf{b}}(\Omega,\lambda)}{\partial\Omega_{m,n}}\right)\right).

(C.9)

We introduce a new adjoint vector
\overrightarrow{\widetilde{\textbf{q}}}
,
defined to be the solution of,

\displaystyle\overleftrightarrow{\textbf{A}}^{T}\overrightarrow{\widetilde{\textbf{q}}}=\frac{\partial G(\Omega,\overrightarrow{\bm{\Phi}})}{\partial\overrightarrow{\bm{\Phi}}}.

(C.10)

Equation (
C.9
) is then used to compute the derivatives of
\chi^{2}_{B}
with respect to
\Omega_{m,n}
,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\chi^{2}_{B}\left(\Omega,\overrightarrow{\bm{\Phi}}(\Omega,\lambda(\Omega))\right)}{\partial\Omega_{m,n}}=\frac{\partial\chi^{2}_{B}(\Omega,\overrightarrow{\bm{\Phi}})}{\partial\Omega_{m,n}}+\frac{\partial\chi^{2}_{B}(\Omega,\overrightarrow{\bm{\Phi}})}{\partial\overrightarrow{\bm{\Phi}}}\cdot\frac{\partial\overrightarrow{\bm{\Phi}}(\Omega,\lambda(\Omega))}{\partial\Omega_{m,n}}.

(C.11)

This result can be written in terms of both adjoint variables,
\overrightarrow{\textbf{q}}
and
\overrightarrow{\widetilde{\textbf{q}}}
,

\frac{\partial\chi^{2}_{B}\left(\Omega,\overrightarrow{\bm{\Phi}}(\Omega,\lambda(\Omega))\right)}{\partial\Omega_{m,n}}=\frac{\partial\chi^{2}_{B}(\Omega,\overrightarrow{\bm{\Phi}})}{\partial\Omega_{m,n}}-\overrightarrow{\textbf{q}}\cdot\left(\frac{\partial\overleftrightarrow{\textbf{A}}(\Omega,\lambda)}{\partial\Omega_{m,n}}\overrightarrow{\bm{\Phi}}-\frac{\partial\overrightarrow{\textbf{b}}(\Omega,\lambda)}{\partial\Omega_{m,n}}\right)\\
-\frac{\overrightarrow{\textbf{q}}\cdot\left(\overleftrightarrow{\textbf{A}}^{K}\overrightarrow{\bm{\Phi}}-\overrightarrow{\textbf{b}}^{K}\right)}{\overrightarrow{\widetilde{\textbf{q}}}\cdot\left(\overleftrightarrow{\textbf{A}}^{K}\overrightarrow{\bm{\Phi}}-\overrightarrow{\textbf{b}}^{K}\right)}\left(\frac{\partial G(\Omega,\overrightarrow{\bm{\Phi}})}{\partial\Omega_{m,n}}-\overrightarrow{\widetilde{\textbf{q}}}\cdot\left(\frac{\partial\overleftrightarrow{\textbf{A}}(\Omega,\lambda)}{\partial\Omega_{m,n}}\overrightarrow{\bm{\Phi}}-\frac{\partial\overrightarrow{\textbf{b}}(\Omega,\lambda)}{\partial\Omega_{m,n}}\right)\right).

(C.12)

The same method is used to compute derivatives of
\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\textbf{J}\right\rVert}_{2}
. So, to obtain the derivatives at fixed
J_{\text{max}}
, we compute a solution to the two adjoint equations, (
3.22
) and (
C.10
), in addition to the forward equation, (
3.8
).

Appendix D:   Trajectory models

In the SFINCS coordinate system, the DKE can be written in the following way,

\dot{\textbf{x}}\cdot\nabla f_{1s}+\dot{X}_{s}\frac{\partial f_{1s}}{\partial X_{s}}+\dot{\xi}_{s}\frac{\partial f_{1s}}{\partial\xi_{s}}-C_{s}(f_{1s})=-\left(\textbf{v}_{\text{m}s}\cdot\nabla\psi\right)\frac{\partial f_{Ms}}{\partial\psi}.

(D.1)

To obtain the trajectory coefficients (
\dot{\textbf{x}}
,
\dot{X}_{s}
, and
\dot{\xi}_{s}
) several approximations are made. For example, any terms that require radial coupling (
\psi
derivatives of
f_{1s}
) cannot be retained, as this would necessitate solving a five-dimensional system.

Under the full trajectory model, the trajectory coefficients are chosen such that
\mu
conservation is maintained as radial coupling is dropped,

\displaystyle\dot{\textbf{x}}

\displaystyle=v_{||}\hat{\textbf{b}}+\frac{\Phi^{\prime}(\psi)}{B^{2}}\textbf{B}\times\nabla\psi

(D.2a)

\displaystyle\dot{X}_{s}

\displaystyle=-\left(\textbf{v}_{\text{m}s}\cdot\nabla\psi\right)\frac{q_{s}}{2T_{s}X_{s}}\Phi^{\prime}(\psi)

(D.2b)

\displaystyle\dot{\xi}_{s}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1-\xi_{s}^{2}}{2B\xi_{s}}v_{||}\hat{\textbf{b}}\cdot\nabla B+\xi_{s}(1-\xi_{s}^{2})\frac{1}{2B^{3}}\Phi^{\prime}(\psi)\textbf{B}\times\nabla\psi\cdot\nabla B.

(D.2c)

Under the DKES trajectory model, the
\textbf{E}\times\textbf{B}
velocity is taken to be divergenceless,

\displaystyle\textbf{v}_{E}^{\text{DKES}}=\frac{\textbf{B}\times\nabla\Phi}{\langle B^{2}\rangle_{\psi}},

(D.3)

where the flux surface average of a quantity is (
4.8
). Under the DKES trajectory model, the trajectory coefficients are taken to be,

\displaystyle\dot{\textbf{x}}

\displaystyle=v_{||}\hat{\textbf{b}}+\frac{1}{\langle B^{2}\rangle_{\psi}}\Phi^{\prime}(\psi)\textbf{B}\times\nabla\psi

(D.4a)

\displaystyle\dot{X}_{s}

\displaystyle=0

(D.4b)

\displaystyle\dot{\xi}_{s}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1-\xi_{s}^{2}}{2B\xi_{s}}v_{||}\hat{\textbf{b}}\cdot\nabla B.

(D.4c)

These effective trajectories are adopted in the widely-used DKES code
[
113
,
230
]
.

Appendix E:   Adjoint collision operator

We want to find an adjoint collision operator,
C_{s}^{\dagger}
, that satisfies the following relation,

\displaystyle\left\langle\int d^{3}v\,\frac{g_{1s}C_{s}(f_{1s})}{f_{Ms}}\right\rangle_{\psi}=\left\langle\int d^{3}v\,\frac{f_{1s}C_{s}^{\dagger}(g_{1s})}{f_{Ms}}\right\rangle_{\psi}.

(E.1)

The linearized Fokker-Planck collision operator can be written as,

\displaystyle C_{s}(f_{1s})=\sum_{s^{\prime}}C_{ss^{\prime}}^{L}(f_{1s},f_{1s^{\prime}})=\sum_{s^{\prime}}C_{ss^{\prime}}(f_{1s},f_{Ms^{\prime}})+C_{ss^{\prime}}(f_{Ms},f_{1s^{\prime}}),

(E.2)

where
s^{\prime}
sums over species. The first term on the right hand side of (
E.2
) is referred to as the test-particle collision operator,
C^{T}_{ss^{\prime}}(f_{1s})=C_{ss^{\prime}}(f_{1s},f_{Ms^{\prime}})
, and the second the field-particle collision operator,
C^{F}_{ss^{\prime}}(f_{1s^{\prime}})=C_{ss^{\prime}}(f_{Ms},f_{1s^{\prime}})
. The test and field terms satisfy the following relations
[
198
,
221
]
,

\displaystyle\int d^{3}v\,\frac{g_{1s}C_{ss^{\prime}}(f_{1s},f_{Ms^{\prime}})}{f_{Ms}}

\displaystyle=\int d^{3}v\,\frac{f_{1s}C_{ss^{\prime}}(g_{1s},f_{Ms^{\prime}})}{f_{Ms}}

(E.3a)

\displaystyle\int d^{3}v\,\frac{g_{1s}C_{ss^{\prime}}(f_{Ms},f_{1s^{\prime}})}{f_{Ms}}

\displaystyle=\frac{T_{s^{\prime}}}{T_{s}}\int d^{3}v\,\frac{f_{1s^{\prime}}C_{s^{\prime}s}(f_{Ms^{\prime}},g_{1s})}{f_{Ms^{\prime}}}.

(E.3b)

For collisions between species of the same temperature, we see that
C_{s}(f_{1s})
is self-adjoint. The adjoint operator with respect to the inner product (
4.30
) is thus,

\displaystyle C_{s}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=C_{s}^{T}+\sum_{s^{\prime}}\frac{f_{Ms}}{f_{Ms^{\prime}}}\frac{T_{s^{\prime}}}{T_{s}}C_{s^{\prime}s}^{F}.

(E.4)

Appendix F:   Adjoint collisionless trajectories

We want to find an adjoint operator,
\mathbb{L}_{0s}^{\dagger}
, that satisfies,

\displaystyle\left\langle\int d^{3}v\,\frac{g_{1s}\mathbb{L}_{0s}f_{1s}}{f_{Ms}}\right\rangle_{\psi}=\left\langle\int d^{3}v\,\frac{f_{1s}\mathbb{L}_{0s}^{\dagger}g_{1s}}{f_{Ms}}\right\rangle_{\psi},

(F.1)

for both trajectory models, where
\mathbb{L}_{0s}
is defined in (
4.10
) with (
D.4
) for the DKES trajectories model and (
D.2
) for the full trajectory model. Throughout we use the velocity space element in SFINCS coordinates,
d^{3}v=2\pi v_{ts}^{3}X_{s}^{2}d\xi_{s}dX_{s}
.

F.0.1
DKES trajectories

The operator under consideration is,

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}_{0s}=v_{||}\hat{\textbf{b}}\cdot\nabla+\hat{\textbf{v}}_{E}^{\text{DKES}}\cdot\nabla-\frac{1-\xi_{s}^{2}}{2B\xi_{s}}v_{||}\hat{\textbf{b}}\cdot\nabla B\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{s}}.

(F.2)

Considering the contribution of the streaming term in (
F.2
) to the left hand side of (
F.1
) we obtain,

\displaystyle\left\langle\int d^{3}v\,\frac{g_{1s}v_{||}\hat{\textbf{b}}\cdot\nabla f_{1s}}{f_{Ms}}\right\rangle_{\psi}=-\left\langle\int d^{3}v\,\frac{f_{1s}v_{||}\textbf{B}\cdot\nabla\left(g_{1s}/B\right)}{f_{Ms}}\right\rangle_{\psi}.

(F.3)

Here the identity
\langle\nabla\cdot\textbf{Q}\rangle_{\psi}=1/V^{\prime}(\psi)\partial/\partial\psi\left(V^{\prime}(\psi)\langle\textbf{Q}\cdot\nabla\psi\rangle_{\psi}\right)
for any vector
Q
has been used. We next consider the contribution of the
\textbf{E}\times\textbf{B}
drift term in (
F.2
),

\displaystyle\left\langle\int d^{3}v\,\frac{g_{1s}\textbf{v}_{E}^{\text{DKES}}\cdot\nabla f_{1s}}{f_{Ms}}\right\rangle_{\psi}

\displaystyle=-\left\langle\int d^{3}v\,\frac{f_{1s}\textbf{v}_{E}^{\text{DKES}}\cdot\nabla g_{1s}}{f_{Ms}}\right\rangle_{\psi}.

(F.4)

Here we have used the identity,

\displaystyle\left\langle\textbf{B}\times\nabla\psi\cdot\nabla w\right\rangle_{\psi}=0,

(F.5)

for any
w
. We consider the contribution of the mirror-force term in (
F.2
),

\displaystyle\left\langle\int d^{3}v\,\frac{g_{1s}\dot{\xi}_{s}}{f_{Ms}}\frac{\partial f_{1s}}{\partial\xi_{s}}\right\rangle_{\psi}=-\left\langle\int d^{3}v\,\frac{f_{1s}\dot{\xi}_{s}}{f_{Ms}}\frac{\partial g_{1s}}{\partial\xi_{s}}\right\rangle_{\psi}-\left\langle\int d^{3}v\,\frac{v_{||}}{B}\hat{\textbf{b}}\cdot\nabla B\frac{g_{1s}f_{1s}}{f_{Ms}}\right\rangle_{\psi}.

(F.6)

Combining (
F.3
-
F.6
), we obtain

\displaystyle\left\langle\int d^{3}v\,\frac{g_{1s}\mathbb{L}_{0s}f_{1s}}{f_{Ms}}\right\rangle_{\psi}=-\left\langle\int d^{3}v\,\frac{f_{1s}\mathbb{L}_{0s}g_{1s}}{f_{Ms}}\right\rangle_{\psi}.

(F.7)

Therefore, in the DKES trajectory model we obtain (
4.47
).

F.0.2
Full trajectories

The operator under consideration for the full model is,

\mathbb{L}_{0s}=v_{||}\hat{\textbf{b}}\cdot\nabla+\textbf{v}_{E}\cdot\nabla+\frac{(1+\xi_{s}^{2})X_{s}}{2B}\textbf{v}_{E}\cdot\nabla B\frac{\partial}{\partial X_{s}}\\
-\frac{1-\xi_{s}^{2}}{2B\xi_{s}}v_{||}\hat{\textbf{b}}\cdot\nabla B\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{s}}+\frac{\xi_{s}(1-\xi_{s}^{2})}{2B}\textbf{v}_{E}\cdot\nabla B\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{s}}.

(F.8)

The contribution to (
F.1
) from the streaming term in (
F.8
) is identical to that in the case of the DKES trajectory model, (
F.3
). We next consider the contribution from the
\textbf{E}\times\textbf{B}
drift term in (
F.8
),

\displaystyle\left\langle\int d^{3}v\,\frac{g_{1s}\textbf{v}_{E}\cdot\nabla f_{1s}}{f_{Ms}}\right\rangle_{\psi}

\displaystyle=-\left\langle\int d^{3}v\,\frac{f_{1s}B^{2}\textbf{v}_{E}\cdot\nabla\left(g_{1s}/B^{2}\right)}{f_{Ms}}\right\rangle_{\psi},

(F.9)

again using (
F.5
). The contribution from the
\dot{X}_{s}
term in (
F.8
) is,

\left\langle\int d^{3}v\,\frac{g_{1s}\dot{X}_{s}}{f_{Ms}}\frac{\partial f_{1s}}{\partial X_{s}}\right\rangle_{\psi}=-\left\langle\int d^{3}v\,\frac{f_{1s}\dot{X}_{s}}{f_{Ms}}\frac{\partial g_{1s}}{\partial X_{s}}\right\rangle_{\psi}\\
-\left\langle\int d^{3}v\,(3+2X_{s}^{2})(1+\xi_{s}^{2})\frac{g_{1s}f_{1s}}{2f_{Ms}B}\textbf{v}_{E}\cdot\nabla B\right\rangle_{\psi}.

(F.10)

The contribution from the mirror term in (
F.8
) is the same as in the case of the DKES trajectories model (
F.6
). We consider the contribution from the final term in (
F.8
),

\left\langle\int d^{3}v\,\frac{g_{1s}\xi_{s}(1-\xi_{s}^{2})\textbf{v}_{E}\cdot\nabla B}{2Bf_{Ms}}\frac{\partial f_{1s}}{\partial\xi_{s}}\right\rangle_{\psi}=-\left\langle\int d^{3}v\,\frac{f_{1s}\xi_{s}(1-\xi_{s}^{2})\textbf{v}_{E}\cdot\nabla B}{2Bf_{Ms}}\frac{\partial g_{1s}}{\partial\xi_{s}}\right\rangle_{\psi}\\
-\left\langle\int d^{3}v\,(1-3\xi_{s}^{2})\textbf{v}_{E}\cdot\nabla B\frac{f_{1s}g_{1s}}{2Bf_{M}}\right\rangle_{\psi}.

(F.11)

Combining (
F.3
), (
F.9
), (
F.10
), (
F.6
), and (
F.11
), we obtain

\left\langle\int d^{3}v\,\frac{g_{1s}\mathbb{L}_{0s}f_{1s}}{f_{Ms}}\right\rangle_{\psi}=-\left\langle\int d^{3}v\,\frac{f_{1s}\mathbb{L}_{0s}g_{1s}}{f_{Ms}}\right\rangle_{\psi}\\
+\Phi^{\prime}(\psi)\frac{q_{s}}{T_{s}}\left\langle\int d^{3}v\,\left(\textbf{v}_{\text{m}s}\cdot\nabla\psi\right)\frac{f_{1s}g_{1s}}{f_{Ms}}\right\rangle_{\psi}.

(F.12)

Therefore, under the full trajectory model we obtain (
4.48
).

Appendix G:   Symmetry of the sensitivity function

In this Appendix we discuss several symmetry properties of the local sensitivity function,
S_{\mathcal{R}}
, defined through (
4.61
). The arguments that follow are similar to those in Appendix C of
[
138
]
. Throughout we will assume that
B
is stellarator symmetric and
N_{P}
symmetric. We will show that this implies
N_{P}
symmetry of
S_{\mathcal{R}}
. In the limit that
E_{r}\rightarrow 0
, then
S_{\mathcal{R}}
also has stellarator symmetry.

G.0.1
Symmetry of
S_{\mathcal{R}}
implied by Fourier derivatives

First we would like to show that
S_{\mathcal{R}}
is stellarator symmetric if and only if
\partial\mathcal{R}/\partial B_{m,n}^{s}=0
for all
m
and
n
, where we express
B
in a Fourier series,

\displaystyle B=\sum_{m,n}B_{m,n}^{c}\cos(m\vartheta_{B}-n\varphi_{B})+B_{m,n}^{s}\sin(m\vartheta_{B}-n\varphi_{B}).

(G.1)

The perturbation,
\delta B
, is decomposed similarly. We begin with the “if” portion of the argument. From (
4.61
) we have,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mathcal{R}}{\partial B_{m,n}^{s}}

\displaystyle=V^{\prime}(\psi)^{-1}\int_{0}^{2\pi}d\vartheta_{B}\int_{0}^{2\pi}d\varphi_{B}\,\sqrt{g}S_{\mathcal{R}}\sin(m\vartheta_{B}-n\varphi_{B}).

(G.2)

Suppose
\partial\mathcal{R}/\partial B_{m,n}^{s}=0
for all
m
and
n
. The quantity
(\sqrt{g}S_{\mathcal{R}})
can be represented as a Fourier series,

\displaystyle\left(\sqrt{g}S_{\mathcal{R}}\right)=\sum_{m,n}A_{m,n}^{c}\cos(m\vartheta_{B}-n\varphi_{B})+A_{m,n}^{s}\sin(m\vartheta_{B}-n\varphi_{B}).

(G.3)

From (
G.2
), we see that
A_{m,n}^{s}=0
for all
m
and
m
.
Thus the quantity
(\sqrt{g}S_{\mathcal{R}})
must be even under the transformation
(\vartheta_{B},\varphi_{B})\rightarrow(-\vartheta_{B},-\varphi_{B})
. We now note that
\sqrt{g}
must be even from (
4.57
) under the assumption that
B
is stellarator symmetric. Therefore
S_{\mathcal{R}}
must be stellarator symmetric, assuming that
\sqrt{g}
does not vanish anywhere, which must be the case for any well-defined coordinate transformation.

We continue with the “only if” portion of the argument. Suppose
S_{\mathcal{R}}
is stellarator symmetric. As
\sqrt{g}
is also stellarator symmetric,
(\sqrt{g}S_{\mathcal{R}})
can be expressed in a Fourier series as (
G.3
) with
A_{m,n}^{s}=0
for all
m
and
n
. Thus from (
G.2
)
\partial\mathcal{R}/\partial B_{m,n}^{s}=0
for all
m
and
n
.

We next show that if
B
is
N_{P}
symmetric, then
S_{\mathcal{R}}
is
N_{P}
symmetric if and only if
\partial\mathcal{R}/\partial B_{m,n}^{c}=0
for all
n
that are not integer multiples of
N_{P}
. We begin with the “if” portion of the argument. From (
4.61
),

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mathcal{R}}{\partial B_{m,n}^{c}}=V^{\prime}(\psi)^{-1}\int_{0}^{2\pi}d\vartheta_{B}\int_{0}^{2\pi}d\varphi_{B}\,\sqrt{g}S_{\mathcal{R}}\cos(m\vartheta_{B}-n\varphi_{B}).

(G.4)

Suppose
\partial\mathcal{R}/\partial B_{m,n}^{c}=0
for all
n
which are not integer multiples of
N_{P}
. Here
(\sqrt{g}S_{\mathcal{R}})
can be expressed in a Fourier series as (
G.3
) with
A_{m,n}^{s}=0
for all
m
and
n
. Inserting the Fourier series into (
G.4
), we find that
A_{m,n}^{c}=0
for all
n
that are not integer multiples of
N_{P}
. Thus
(\sqrt{g}S_{\mathcal{R}})
must be
N_{P}
symmetric. As
\sqrt{g}
must be
N_{P}
symmetric, this implies
S_{\mathcal{R}}
possesses the same symmetry.

Next we consider the “only if” portion of the argument. Suppose that
S_{\mathcal{R}}
is
N_{P}
symmetric. As
\sqrt{g}
is also
N_{P}
symmetric, then
(\sqrt{g}S_{\mathcal{R}})
can be expressed in a Fourier series as (
G.3
) where the sum includes
n
that are integer multiples of
N_{P}
. Inserting the Fourier series into (
G.4
), we find that
\partial\mathcal{R}/\partial B_{m,n}^{c}=0
for all
n
that are not integer multiples of
N_{P}
.

G.0.2
Symmetry of Fourier derivatives

To continue, we need to show that
\partial\mathcal{R}/\partial B_{m,n}^{s}=0
for all
m
and
n
and
\partial\mathcal{R}/\partial B_{m,n}^{c}=0
for all
n
which are not integer multiples of
N_{P}
. We begin with the
N_{P}
symmetry argument. We consider the symmetry of
f_{1s}
implied by (
D.1
). Under the transformation
\varphi_{B}\rightarrow\varphi_{B}+2\pi/N_{P}
, we find that each of the trajectory coefficients remain unchanged, as well as the source term and collision operator. Therefore we can conclude that
f_{1s}
is
N_{P}
symmetric. We can also note that each of the
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}
vectors are
N_{P}
symmetric, as well as
\sqrt{g}
. We consider the integrand that appears in the flux surface average in (
4.32
),

\displaystyle D_{s}(\vartheta_{B},\varphi_{B})=\int d^{3}v\,\frac{f_{1s}\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{s}^{f}\sqrt{g}}{f_{Ms}}.

(G.5)

Here the superscript and subscript on
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}
denotes that we consider the unknowns corresponding to the distribution function of species
s
. We note that
D_{s}(\vartheta_{B},\varphi_{B}+2\pi/N_{P})=D_{s}(\vartheta_{B},\varphi_{B})
. The quantity
\mathcal{R}
can be expressed in terms of
D_{s}
as follows,

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}=\sum_{s}V^{\prime}(\psi)^{-1}\int_{0}^{2\pi}d\varphi_{B}\int_{0}^{2\pi}d\vartheta_{B}\,D_{s}.

(G.6)

Next we consider the functional derivative of
\mathcal{R}
with respect to
B
, defined as in (
4.60
). The derivative with respect to
B_{m,n}^{c}
can be thus defined as,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mathcal{R}}{\partial B_{m,n}^{c}}=V^{\prime}(\psi)^{-1}\int_{0}^{2\pi}d\varphi_{B}\int_{0}^{2\pi}d\vartheta_{B}\,\left(\sum_{s}\frac{\delta D_{s}}{\delta B}-\mathcal{R}\frac{\delta\sqrt{g}}{\delta B}\right)\cos(m\vartheta_{B}-n\varphi_{B}).

(G.7)

As the functional derivative maintains the
N_{P}
symmetry of
D_{s}
and
\sqrt{g}
, the quantity in parenthesis in (
G.7
) can be expressed in a Fourier series containing only
n
that are integer multiples of
N_{P}
. Thus we see that the quantity
\partial\mathcal{R}/\partial B_{m,n}^{c}=0
for all
n
that are not integer multiples of
N_{P}
.

Next we consider a similar argument for stellarator symmetry. We begin by considering the symmetry of
f_{1s}
implied by (
D.1
) in the case
E_{r}=0
. Under the transformation
(\vartheta_{B},\varphi_{B},v_{||})\rightarrow(-\vartheta_{B},-\varphi_{B},-v_{||})
, we see that both the collisionless trajectory operator and the collision operator maintain the parity of
f_{1s}
, while the source term is odd. Therefore,
f_{1s}
must be odd under this transformation. In this case, we can write
f_{1s}
as,

\displaystyle f_{1s}=f^{-}_{a,s}(X_{s},\xi_{s})f^{+}_{b,s}(\vartheta_{B},\varphi_{B})+f^{+}_{a,s}(X_{s},\xi_{s})f^{-}_{b,s}(\vartheta_{B},\varphi_{B}),

(G.8)

where
f^{-}_{a,s}(X_{s},-\xi_{s})=-f^{-}_{a,s}(X_{s},\xi_{s})
,
f^{+}_{a,s}(X_{s},-\xi_{s})=f^{+}_{a,s}(X_{s},-\xi_{s})
, and analogous expressions for
f_{b,s}^{+}
and
f_{b,s}^{-}
.

We next note that each of the
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{f}_{s}
are odd under the transformation
(\vartheta_{B},\varphi_{B},v_{||})\rightarrow(-\vartheta_{B},-\varphi_{B},-v_{||})
. As
\sqrt{g}
is even, then we can express
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{s}^{f}\sqrt{g}
in a similar way to (
G.8
),

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{s}^{f}\sqrt{g}=B_{a,s}^{-}(X_{s},\xi_{s})B_{b,s}^{+}(\vartheta_{B},\varphi_{B})+B_{a,s}^{+}(X_{s},\xi_{s})B_{b,s}^{-}(\vartheta_{B},\varphi_{B}).

(G.9)

The integrand that appears in the flux surface average becomes,

D_{s}=\int d^{3}v\,f_{Ms}^{-1}\bigg(f_{a,s}^{-}(X_{s},\xi_{s})B_{a,s}^{-}(X_{s},\xi_{s})f_{b,s}^{+}(\vartheta_{B},\varphi_{B})B_{b,s}^{+}(\vartheta_{B},\varphi_{B})\\
+f_{a,s}^{+}(X_{s},\xi_{s})B_{a,s}^{+}(X_{s},\xi_{s})f_{b,s}^{-}(\vartheta_{B},\varphi_{B})B_{b,s}^{-}(\vartheta_{B},\varphi_{B})\bigg).

(G.10)

We see that
D_{s}
is even with respect to the transformation
(\vartheta_{B},\varphi_{B})\rightarrow(-\vartheta_{B},-\varphi_{B})
.
The quantity
\mathcal{R}
can be written as in (
G.6
) and the derivative with respect to a stellarator asymmetric mode is

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mathcal{R}}{\partial B_{m,n}^{s}}=V^{\prime}(\psi)^{-1}\int_{0}^{2\pi}d\varphi_{B}\int_{0}^{2\pi}d\vartheta_{B}\,\left(\sum_{s}\frac{\delta D_{s}}{\delta B}-\mathcal{R}\frac{\delta\sqrt{g}}{\delta B}\right)\sin(m\vartheta_{B}-n\varphi_{B}).

(G.11)

The functional derivative with respect to
B
does not change the parity of
D_{s}
or
\sqrt{g}
, thus we see that the quantity in parenthesis in the above equation is even with respect to the transformation
(\vartheta_{B},\varphi_{B})\rightarrow(-\vartheta_{B},-\varphi_{B})
. Therefore,
\partial\mathcal{R}/\partial B_{m,n}^{s}=0
for all
m
and
n
. A similar argument cannot be made if
E_{r}\neq 0
, as the inhomogeneous drive term in (
D.1
) no longer has definite parity. However, according to the arguments in
[
112
]
the transport coefficients do obey this symmetry property.

Appendix H:   Derivatives at ambipolarity

In this Appendix, we derive an expression for derivatives of moments of the distribution function at fixed ambipolarity rather than fixed
E_{r}
by determining the relationship between geometry parameters,
\Omega
, and
E_{r}
. We begin by assuming that the continuous adjoint approach outlined in Section
4.3.1
is used. The approach taken here is analogous to that used in Appendix
C
, in which an additional adjoint equation is used to compute derivatives at a fixed constraint function for optimization of stellarator coil shapes.

Consider the set of unknowns computed with SFINCS,
F
, which depends on parameters
\Omega
and
E_{r}
. The total differential of
F
satisfies,

\mathbb{L}dF(\Omega,E_{r})=\left(\frac{\partial\mathbb{S}(\Omega,E_{r})}{\partial E_{r}}-\frac{\partial\mathbb{L}(\Omega,E_{r})}{\partial E_{r}}F\right)dE_{r}\\
+\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\Omega}}\left(\frac{\partial\mathbb{S}(\Omega,E_{r})}{\partial\Omega_{i}}-\frac{\partial\mathbb{L}(\Omega,E_{r})}{\partial\Omega_{i}}F\right)d\Omega_{i},

(H.1)

which follows from (
4.29
). Consider
J_{r}(\Omega,F)
, which depends on
E_{r}
through
F
. The total differential of
J_{r}
can be computed,

\displaystyle dJ_{r}(\Omega,F(\Omega,E_{r}))=\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\Omega}}\frac{\partial J_{r}\left(\Omega,F\right)}{\partial\Omega_{i}}d\Omega_{i}+\left\langle\widetilde{J_{r}},dF(\Omega,E_{r})\right\rangle,

(H.2)

which can be written using (
H.1
) and the solution to (
4.70
),

dJ_{r}(\Omega,F(\Omega,E_{r}))=\left\langle\lambda^{J_{r}},\left(\frac{\partial\mathbb{L}(\Omega,E_{r})}{\partial E_{r}}F-\frac{\partial\mathbb{S}(\Omega,E_{r})}{\partial E_{r}}\right)\right\rangle dE_{r}\\
+\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\Omega}}\left(\frac{\partial J_{r}(\Omega,F)}{\partial\Omega_{i}}+\left\langle\lambda^{J_{r}},\left(\frac{\partial\mathbb{L}(\Omega,E_{r})}{\partial\Omega_{i}}F-\frac{\partial\mathbb{S}(\Omega,E_{r})}{\partial\Omega_{i}}\right)\right\rangle\right)d\Omega_{i}.

(H.3)

By enforcing
dJ_{r}(\Omega,F(\Omega,E_{r}))=0
, we obtain the relationship between
E_{r}
and
\Omega
at ambipolarity,

\frac{\partial E_{r}(\Omega)}{\partial\Omega_{i}}\bigg\rvert_{dJ_{r}=0}=-\left\langle\lambda^{J_{r}},\left(\frac{\partial\mathbb{L}(\Omega,E_{r})}{\partial E_{r}}F-\frac{\partial\mathbb{S}(\Omega,E_{r})}{\partial E_{r}}\right)\right\rangle^{-1}\\
\left(\frac{\partial J_{r}(\Omega,F)}{\partial\Omega_{i}}+\left\langle\lambda^{J_{r}},\left(\frac{\partial\mathbb{L}(\Omega,E_{r})}{\partial\Omega_{i}}F-\frac{\partial\mathbb{S}(\Omega,E_{r})}{\partial\Omega_{i}}\right)\right\rangle\right).

(H.4)

Consider a moment of the distribution function,
\mathcal{R}(\Omega,F(\Omega,E_{r}))
. The derivative with respect to
\Omega_{i}
at fixed ambipolarity can thus be computed,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mathcal{R}(\Omega,F(\Omega,E_{r}(\Omega))}{\partial\Omega_{i}}=\frac{\partial\mathcal{R}(\Omega,F)}{\partial\Omega_{i}}+\left\langle\widetilde{\mathcal{R}},\frac{\partial F(\Omega,E_{r}(\Omega))}{\partial\Omega_{i}}\right\rangle,

(H.5)

where
E_{r}
is viewed as a function of
\Omega
through (
H.4
). The first term corresponds to the explicit dependence on
\Omega_{i}
, while the second contains dependence through
F
.
Here
\partial F(\Omega,E_{r}(\Omega))/\partial\Omega_{i}
satisfies,

\mathbb{L}\frac{\partial F(\Omega,E_{r}(\Omega))}{\partial\Omega_{i}}=\left(\frac{\partial\mathbb{S}(\Omega,E_{r})}{\partial\Omega_{i}}-\frac{\partial\mathbb{L}(\Omega,E_{r})}{\partial\Omega_{i}}F\right)\\
-\left(\frac{\partial\mathbb{S}(\Omega,E_{r})}{\partial E_{r}}-\frac{\partial\mathbb{L}(\Omega,E_{r})}{\partial E_{r}}F\right)\left\langle\lambda^{J_{r}},\left(\frac{\partial\mathbb{L}(\Omega,E_{r})}{\partial E_{r}}F-\frac{\partial\mathbb{S}(\Omega,E_{r})}{\partial E_{r}}\right)\right\rangle^{-1}\\
\times\left(\frac{\partial J_{r}(\Omega,F)}{\partial\Omega_{i}}+\left\langle\lambda^{J_{r}},\left(\frac{\partial\mathbb{L}(\Omega,E_{r})}{\partial\Omega_{i}}F-\frac{\partial\mathbb{S}(\Omega,E_{r})}{\partial\Omega_{i}}\right)\right\rangle\right),

(H.6)

from (
H.1
) using (
H.4
).
Using (
H.6
) and (
4.41
), we find

\frac{\partial\mathcal{R}(\Omega,F(\Omega,E_{r}(\Omega))}{\partial\Omega_{i}}=\frac{\partial\mathcal{R}(\Omega,F)}{\partial\Omega_{i}}+\left\langle\lambda^{\mathcal{R}},\left(\frac{\partial\mathbb{L}(\Omega,E_{r})}{\partial\Omega_{i}}F-\frac{\partial\mathbb{S}(\Omega,E_{r})}{\partial\Omega_{i}}\right)\right\rangle\\
-\left\langle\lambda^{\mathcal{R}},\left(\frac{\partial\mathbb{L}(\Omega,E_{r})}{\partial E_{r}}F-\frac{\partial\mathbb{S}(\Omega,E_{r})}{\partial E_{r}}\right)\right\rangle\\
\times\frac{\left(\frac{\partial J_{r}(\Omega,F)}{\partial\Omega_{i}}+\left\langle\lambda^{J_{r}},\left(\frac{\partial\mathbb{L}(\Omega,E_{r})}{\partial\Omega_{i}}F-\frac{\partial\mathbb{S}(\Omega,E_{r})}{\partial\Omega_{i}}\right)\right\rangle\right)}{\left\langle\lambda^{J_{r}},\left(\frac{\partial\mathbb{L}(\Omega,E_{r})}{\partial E_{r}}F-\frac{\partial\mathbb{S}(\Omega,E_{r})}{\partial E_{r}}\right)\right\rangle}.

(H.7)

An analogous expression can be obtained using the discrete approach,

\frac{\partial\mathcal{R}\left(\Omega,\overrightarrow{\textbf{F}}\left(\Omega,E_{r}(\Omega)\right)\right)}{\partial\Omega_{i}}=\frac{\partial\mathcal{R}\left(\Omega,\overrightarrow{\textbf{F}}\right)}{\partial\Omega_{i}}+\left\langle\overrightarrow{\bm{\lambda}}^{\mathcal{R}},\left(\frac{\partial\overrightarrow{\textbf{S}}(\Omega,E_{r})}{\partial\Omega_{i}}-\frac{\partial\overleftrightarrow{\textbf{L}}(\Omega,E_{r})}{\partial\Omega_{i}}\overrightarrow{\textbf{F}}\right)\right\rangle\\
-\left\langle\overrightarrow{\bm{\lambda}}^{\mathcal{R}},\left(\frac{\partial\overrightarrow{\textbf{S}}(\Omega,E_{r})}{\partial E_{r}}-\frac{\partial\overleftrightarrow{\textbf{L}}(\Omega,E_{r})}{\partial E_{r}}\overrightarrow{\textbf{F}}\right)\right\rangle\\
\times\frac{\left(\frac{\partial J_{r}\left(\Omega,\overrightarrow{\textbf{F}}\right)}{\partial\Omega_{i}}+\left\langle\overrightarrow{\bm{\lambda}}^{J_{r}},\left(\frac{\partial\overrightarrow{\textbf{S}}(\Omega,E_{r})}{\partial\Omega_{i}}-\frac{\partial\overleftrightarrow{\textbf{L}}(\Omega,E_{r})}{\partial\Omega_{i}}\overrightarrow{\textbf{F}}\right)\right\rangle\right)}{\left\langle\overrightarrow{\bm{\lambda}}^{J_{r}},\left(\frac{\partial\overrightarrow{\textbf{S}}(\Omega,E_{r})}{\partial E_{r}}-\frac{\partial\overleftrightarrow{\textbf{L}}(\Omega,E_{r})}{\partial E_{r}}\overrightarrow{\textbf{F}}\right)\right\rangle},

(H.8)

where (
4.71
) has been used.

Appendix I:   Derivation of generalized MHD self-adjointness relation

The quantity
U_{P}=U_{P_{1}}+U_{P_{2}}
consists of two terms, accounting for changes to the vector potential due to MHD perturbations,

\displaystyle U_{P_{1}}=\int_{V_{P}}d^{3}x\,\left(\delta\textbf{J}_{1}\cdot\bm{\xi}_{2}\times\textbf{B}-\delta\textbf{J}_{2}\cdot\bm{\xi}_{1}\times\textbf{B}\right),

(I.1)

and changes to the rotational transform,

\displaystyle U_{P_{2}}=\int_{V_{P}}d^{3}x\,\left(\delta\chi_{1}(\psi)\delta\textbf{J}_{2}\cdot\nabla\varphi-\delta\chi_{2}(\psi)\delta\textbf{J}_{1}\cdot\nabla\varphi\right).

(I.2)

The quantity
U_{P_{1}}
can be expressed by using (
5.26
) and applying the divergence theorem to the pressure gradient terms,

U_{P_{1}}=\int_{V_{P}}d^{3}x\,\bm{\xi}_{2}\cdot\left(\textbf{J}\times\delta\textbf{B}_{1}+\nabla p\left(\nabla\cdot\bm{\xi}_{1}\right)-\textbf{F}_{1}\right)\\
-\int_{V_{P}}d^{3}x\,\bm{\xi}_{1}\cdot\left(\textbf{J}\times\delta\textbf{B}_{2}+\nabla p\left(\nabla\cdot\bm{\xi}_{2}\right)-\textbf{F}_{2}\right).

(I.3)

We will define
\delta\widetilde{\textbf{B}}_{1,2}=\nabla\times\left(\bm{\xi}_{1,2}\times\textbf{B}\right)
such that
\delta\textbf{B}_{1,2}=\delta\widetilde{\textbf{B}}_{1,2}-\nabla\delta\chi_{1,2}(\psi)\times\nabla\varphi
.
The terms in (
I.3
) due to
\delta\widetilde{\textbf{B}}_{1,2}
can be evaluated using
\textbf{J}=J_{||}\hat{\textbf{b}}+\hat{\textbf{b}}\times\nabla p/B
and (
5.10
),

\int_{V_{P}}d^{3}x\,\left(\bm{\xi}_{2}\cdot\textbf{J}\times\delta\widetilde{\textbf{B}}_{1}-\bm{\xi}_{1}\cdot\textbf{J}\times\delta\widetilde{\textbf{B}}_{2}\right)=\int_{V_{P}}d^{3}x\,\frac{J_{||}}{B}\nabla\cdot\left(\left(\bm{\xi}_{1}\times\textbf{B}\right)\times\left(\bm{\xi}_{2}\times\textbf{B}\right)\right)\\
+\int_{V_{P}}d^{3}x\,\frac{1}{B}\left(\left(\bm{\xi}_{2}\cdot\nabla p\right)\hat{\textbf{b}}\cdot\delta\widetilde{\textbf{B}}_{1}-\left(\bm{\xi}_{1}\cdot\nabla p\right)\hat{\textbf{b}}\cdot\delta\widetilde{\textbf{B}}_{2}\right).

(I.4)

The first term in (
I.4
) can be simplified using
\nabla\cdot\textbf{J}=0
and noting that the perturbation can be written as
\bm{\xi}_{1,2}=\xi^{\psi}_{1,2}\nabla\psi+\xi^{\perp}_{1,2}\hat{\textbf{b}}\times\nabla\psi
.
Applying the identity
\textbf{B}\cdot\delta\widetilde{\textbf{B}}_{1,2}=-B^{2}\nabla\cdot\bm{\xi}_{1,2}-\bm{\xi}_{1,2}\cdot\nabla B^{2}-\mu_{0}\bm{\xi}_{1,2}\cdot\nabla p
to the second term, the following expression can be obtained,

\int_{V_{P}}d^{3}x\,\left(\bm{\xi}_{2}\cdot\textbf{J}\times\delta\widetilde{\textbf{B}}_{1}-\bm{\xi}_{1}\cdot\textbf{J}\times\delta\widetilde{\textbf{B}}_{2}\right)=\\
\int_{V_{P}}d^{3}x\,\left(\left(\nabla\cdot\bm{\xi}_{2}\right)\bm{\xi}_{1}\cdot\nabla p-\left(\nabla\cdot\bm{\xi}_{1}\right)\bm{\xi}_{2}\cdot\nabla p\right).

(I.5)

Hence we obtain the following expression for
U_{P_{1}}
,

U_{P_{1}}=\int_{V_{P}}d^{3}x\,\left(-\bm{\xi}_{2}\cdot\textbf{F}_{1}+\bm{\xi}_{1}\cdot\textbf{F}_{2}\right)\\
-\int_{V_{P}}d^{3}x\,\left(\delta\chi_{1}^{\prime}(\psi)\bm{\xi}_{2}\cdot\nabla\psi-\delta\chi_{2}^{\prime}(\psi)\bm{\xi}_{1}\cdot\nabla\psi\right)\textbf{J}\cdot\nabla\varphi.

(I.6)

We now consider
U_{P_{2}}
defined in (
I.2
).
Applying (
5.24
) for the change in toroidal current, integrating by parts in
\psi
, and combining the expressions for
U_{P_{1}}
(
I.3
) and
U_{P_{2}}
(
I.2
), we obtain,

U_{P}=\int_{V_{P}}d^{3}x\,\left(-\bm{\xi}_{2}\cdot\textbf{F}_{1}+\bm{\xi}_{1}\cdot\textbf{F}_{2}\right)+2\pi\int_{V_{P}}d\psi\,\left(\delta\chi_{1}(\psi)\delta I_{T,2}^{\prime}(\psi)-\delta\chi_{2}(\psi)\delta I_{T,1}^{\prime}(\psi)\right)\\
-\int_{S_{P}}d^{2}x\,\left(\delta\chi_{1}(\psi)\bm{\xi}_{2}-\delta\chi_{2}(\psi)\bm{\xi}_{1}\right)\cdot\hat{\textbf{n}}\textbf{J}\cdot\nabla\varphi.

(I.7)

Next we combine
U_{P}
(
I.7
) with
U_{B}
(
5.31
) and
U_{C}
(
5.32
) to obtain the free-boundary adjoint relation (
5.33
).

To obtain the fixed-boundary adjoint relation, the integral over the plasma volume (
5.29
) can be related to a surface integral by applying the divergence theorem to arrive at
eq.

5.35
. Using (
5.19
) and applying several vector identities,

U_{P}=-\frac{1}{\mu_{0}}\int_{S_{P}}d^{2}x\,\hat{\textbf{n}}\cdot\left(\bm{\xi}_{1}\delta\textbf{B}_{2}-\bm{\xi}_{2}\delta\textbf{B}_{1}\right)\cdot\textbf{B}\\
-\frac{1}{\mu_{0}}\int_{S_{P}}d^{2}x\,\left(\delta\chi_{2}(\psi)\delta\textbf{B}_{1}-\delta\chi_{1}(\psi)\delta\textbf{B}_{2}\right)\cdot\nabla\varphi\times\hat{\textbf{n}}.

(I.8)

Using (
I.7
) and expressing the second term in (
I.8
) as a perturbed current using (
5.24
), the fixed boundary adjoint relation (
5.36
) is obtained.

Appendix J:   Alternate derivation of fixed-boundary adjoint relation

The MHD force operator,

\displaystyle\textbf{F}[\bm{\xi}_{1,2}]=\textbf{J}\times\left(\nabla\times\left(\bm{\xi}_{1,2}\times\textbf{B}\right)\right)+\frac{\nabla\times\left(\nabla\times\left(\bm{\xi}_{1,2}\times\textbf{B}\right)\right)\times\textbf{B}}{\mu_{0}}+\nabla\left(\bm{\xi}_{1,2}\cdot\nabla p\right),

(J.1)

possesses the following self-adjointness property
[
20
,
83
]
,

\displaystyle\int_{V_{P}}d^{3}x\,\left(\bm{\xi}_{2}\cdot\textbf{F}[\bm{\xi}_{1}]-\bm{\xi}_{1}\cdot\textbf{F}[\bm{\xi}_{2}]\right)=\frac{1}{\mu_{0}}\int_{S_{P}}d^{2}x\,\hat{\textbf{n}}\cdot\left(\bm{\xi}_{1}\textbf{B}\cdot\delta\widetilde{\textbf{B}}_{2}-\bm{\xi}_{2}\textbf{B}\cdot\delta\widetilde{\textbf{B}}_{1}\right),

(J.2)

where
\delta\widetilde{\textbf{B}}_{1,2}=\nabla\times\left(\bm{\xi}_{1,2}\times\textbf{B}\right)
is the perturbed field corresponding to the MHD perturbations. As we consider linearized equilibrium states that preserve
p(\psi)
, the perturbed pressure satisfies
\delta p(\psi)=-\bm{\xi}\cdot\nabla p
. The force operator we adopt (
J.1
) is the
\gamma\rightarrow 0
limit of the more general form of the force operator (
5.9
), which sometimes includes the term
\nabla\left(\gamma p\nabla\cdot\bm{\xi}\right)
.

For perturbations described by
eqs.

5.19
,
5.20
,
5.23
,
5.24
,
5.25
and
5.26
, the force operator satisfies,

\displaystyle\textbf{F}[\bm{\xi}_{1,2}]=\textbf{J}\times\left(\nabla\delta\chi_{1,2}(\psi)\times\nabla\varphi\right)+\frac{\nabla\times\left(\nabla\delta\chi_{1,2}(\psi)\times\nabla\varphi\right)\times\textbf{B}}{\mu_{0}}-\delta\textbf{F}_{1,2}.

(J.3)

Using
eq.

J.3
and several vector identities, the left hand side of
eq.

J.2
can be written as

\int_{V_{P}}d^{3}x\,\left(\bm{\xi}_{2}\cdot\textbf{F}[\bm{\xi}_{1}]-\bm{\xi}_{1}\cdot\textbf{F}[\bm{\xi}_{2}]\right)=\int_{V_{P}}d^{3}x\,\left(\delta\chi_{1}^{\prime}(\psi)\bm{\xi}_{2}-\delta\chi_{2}^{\prime}(\psi)\bm{\xi}_{1}\right)\cdot\nabla\psi\textbf{J}\cdot\nabla\varphi\\
-\frac{1}{\mu_{0}}\int_{V_{P}}d^{3}x\,\nabla\psi\times\nabla\varphi\cdot\left(\delta\chi_{1}^{\prime}(\psi)\delta\widetilde{\textbf{B}}_{2}-\delta\chi_{2}^{\prime}(\psi)\delta\widetilde{\textbf{B}}_{1}\right)\\
-\frac{1}{\mu_{0}}\int_{S_{P}}d^{2}x\,\left(\bm{\xi}_{2}\delta\chi_{1}^{\prime}(\psi)-\bm{\xi}_{1}\delta\chi_{2}^{\prime}(\psi)\right)\cdot\hat{\textbf{n}}\left(\nabla\psi\times\nabla\varphi\cdot\textbf{B}\right)\\
-\int_{V_{P}}d^{3}x\,\left(\bm{\xi}_{2}\cdot\delta\textbf{F}_{1}-\bm{\xi}_{1}\cdot\delta\textbf{F}_{2}\right).

(J.4)

In arriving at (
J.4
), we use
\textbf{J}\cdot\nabla\psi=0
, which follow from MHD force balance (
5.10
).
Using
eq.

5.24
to re-express the first two terms on the right-hand side,

\int_{V_{P}}d^{3}x\,\left(\bm{\xi}_{2}\cdot\textbf{F}[\bm{\xi}_{1}]-\bm{\xi}_{1}\cdot\textbf{F}[\bm{\xi}_{2}]\right)=2\pi\int_{V_{P}}d\psi\,\left(\delta I_{T,2}(\psi)\delta\chi_{1}^{\prime}(\psi)-\delta I_{T,1}(\psi)\delta\chi_{2}^{\prime}(\psi)\right)\\
-\frac{1}{\mu_{0}}\int_{S_{P}}d^{2}x\,\left(\bm{\xi}_{2}\delta\chi_{1}^{\prime}(\psi)-\bm{\xi}_{1}\delta\chi_{2}^{\prime}(\psi)\right)\cdot\hat{\textbf{n}}\left(\nabla\psi\times\nabla\varphi\cdot\textbf{B}\right)\\
-\int_{V_{P}}d^{3}x\,\left(\bm{\xi}_{2}\cdot\delta\textbf{F}_{1}-\bm{\xi}_{1}\cdot\delta\textbf{F}_{2}\right).

(J.5)

Using
eqs.

5.19
and
J.2
we obtain
eq.

5.36
.

Appendix K:   Interpretation of the displacement vector

For MHD perturbations such that
\delta\textbf{B}=\nabla\times\left(\bm{\xi}\times\textbf{B}\right)
the displacement can be interpreted as a vector describing the motion of a field lines. Thus a normal perturbation to the surface of the plasma as in (
5.4
) can be expressed in terms of the displacement vector,

\displaystyle\delta f(S_{P};\bm{\xi})=\int_{S_{P}}d^{2}x\,\mathcal{G}\bm{\xi}\cdot\hat{\textbf{n}}.

(K.1)

For perturbations that allow for changes in the rotational transform it remains to be shown that a similar relation can be found.

As we require that
\psi
remain a flux surface label in the perturbed equilibrium, the Lagrangian perturbation to
\psi
at fixed position is

\displaystyle\delta\psi=-\delta\textbf{x}\cdot\nabla\psi.

(K.2)

The perturbed magnetic field,
\textbf{B}^{\prime}=\textbf{B}+\delta\textbf{B}
must remain tangent to
\psi^{\prime}=\psi+\delta\psi
surfaces; thus to first order in the perturbation,

\displaystyle 0=\textbf{B}^{\prime}\cdot\nabla\psi^{\prime}=\textbf{B}\cdot\nabla\delta\psi+\delta\textbf{B}\cdot\nabla\psi.

(K.3)

Applying the form for the perturbed field allowing for changes in the rotational transform,
\delta\textbf{B}=\nabla\times\left(\bm{\xi}\times\textbf{B}-\delta\chi(\psi)\nabla\varphi\right)
, and using several vector identities, the following condition is obtained

\displaystyle\textbf{B}\cdot\nabla\left(\delta\textbf{x}\cdot\nabla\psi\right)=\textbf{B}\cdot\nabla\left(\bm{\xi}\cdot\nabla\psi\right).

(K.4)

This implies that
\delta\textbf{x}\cdot\nabla\psi=\bm{\xi}\cdot\nabla\psi+F(\psi)
, where
F(\psi)
is some flux function which can be determined by requiring that the perturbation to the toroidal flux as a function of
\psi
vanishes,
\delta\Psi_{T}(\psi)=0
.

The perturbed toroidal flux through a surface labeled by
\psi
contains two terms, corresponding to the flux of the unperturbed field through the perturbed surface and the perturbed field through the unperturbed surface,

\displaystyle\delta\Psi_{T}(\psi)=\int_{\partial S_{T}(\psi)}d\vartheta\,\sqrt{g}\delta\textbf{x}\cdot\nabla\psi\textbf{B}\cdot\nabla\varphi+\int_{S_{T}(\psi)}d\psi d\vartheta\,\sqrt{g}\delta\textbf{B}\cdot\nabla\varphi.

(K.5)

Using the form for
\delta\textbf{B}
, applying the divergence theorem, and noting that
\textbf{B}\cdot\nabla\varphi=\sqrt{g}^{-1}
, the following condition is obtained,

\displaystyle\delta\Psi_{T}(\psi)=\int_{0}^{2\pi}d\vartheta\,\left(\delta\textbf{x}\cdot\nabla\psi-\bm{\xi}\cdot\nabla\psi\right).

(K.6)

By requiring that
\delta\Psi_{T}(\psi)=0
, we find that
F(\psi)=0
. Thus we can express shape gradients in the form of (
K.1
) even when the rotational transform is allowed to vary.

Appendix L:   Details of axis ripple calculation

In this Appendix, we compute the shape derivative of the finite-pressure magnetic well figure of merit from (
5.101
) and show that if we impose an adjoint perturbation of the form (
5.102
), the shape gradient is given by (
5.106
).

We use the expression for the perturbation to the field strength (
5.62
) and
\delta\psi=-\bm{\xi}_{1}\cdot\nabla\psi
with (
5.101
) to obtain,

\delta f_{R}(S_{P};\bm{\xi}_{1})=\int_{S_{P}}d^{2}x\,\bm{\xi}_{1}\cdot\hat{\textbf{n}}\widetilde{f_{R}}-\int_{V_{P}}d^{3}x\,\frac{\partial\widetilde{f_{R}}}{\partial\psi}\bm{\xi}_{1}\cdot\nabla\psi\\
-\int_{V_{P}}d^{3}x\,\frac{\partial\widetilde{f_{R}}}{\partial B}\frac{1}{B}\left(B^{2}\nabla\cdot\bm{\xi}_{1}+\bm{\xi}_{1}\cdot\nabla\left(B^{2}+\mu_{0}p\right)+\delta\chi_{1}^{\prime}(\psi)\textbf{B}\cdot\left(\nabla\psi\times\nabla\varphi\right)\right).

(L.1)

The third term can be integrated by parts to obtain,

\delta f_{R}(S_{P};\bm{\xi}_{1})=\int_{S_{P}}d^{2}x\,\bm{\xi}_{1}\cdot\hat{\textbf{n}}\left(\widetilde{f_{R}}-\frac{\partial\widetilde{f_{R}}}{\partial B}B\right)+\int_{V_{P}}d^{3}x\,\left(\frac{\partial^{2}\widetilde{f_{R}}}{\partial B\partial\psi}B-\frac{\partial\widetilde{f_{R}}}{\partial\psi}\right)\bm{\xi}_{1}\cdot\nabla\psi\\
+\int_{V_{P}}d^{3}x\,\left(-\frac{\partial\widetilde{f_{R}}}{\partial B}B\bm{\xi}_{1}\cdot\bm{\kappa}+B\frac{\partial^{2}\widetilde{f_{R}}}{\partial B^{2}}\bm{\xi}_{1}\cdot\nabla B+\delta\chi_{1}^{\prime}(\psi)\frac{\partial\widetilde{f_{R}}}{\partial B}\hat{\textbf{b}}\cdot\left(\nabla\varphi\times\nabla\psi\right)\right),

(L.2)

where the expression for the curvature in an equilibrium field (
5.105
) has been applied.

We compute one term that appears in the fixed-boundary adjoint relation (
5.36
) using the prescribed adjoint bulk force perturbation (
5.102a
),

\int_{V_{P}}d^{3}x\,\bm{\xi}_{1}\cdot\textbf{F}_{2}=\int_{V_{P}}d^{3}x\,\left(-\frac{\partial^{2}p_{||}}{\partial B\partial\psi}B+\frac{\partial p_{||}}{\partial\psi}\right)\bm{\xi}_{1}\cdot\nabla\psi\\
+\int_{V_{P}}d^{3}x\,\left(\frac{\partial p_{||}}{\partial B}B\bm{\xi}_{1}\cdot\bm{\kappa}-B\frac{\partial^{2}p_{||}}{\partial B^{2}}\bm{\xi}_{1}\cdot\nabla B\right),

(L.3)

where we have applied the parallel force balance condition (
5.103
).
Therefore, if we impose
p_{||}=\widetilde{f_{R}}
, we obtain the following expression for the shape derivative of
f_{R}
,

\delta f_{R}(S_{P};\bm{\xi}_{1})=\int_{S_{P}}d^{2}x\,\bm{\xi}_{1}\cdot\hat{\textbf{n}}\left(\widetilde{f_{R}}-\frac{\partial\widetilde{f_{R}}}{\partial B}B\right)-\int_{V_{P}}d^{3}x\,\bm{\xi}_{1}\cdot\textbf{F}_{2}\\
+\int_{V_{P}}d^{3}x\,\delta\chi_{1}^{\prime}(\psi)\frac{\partial\widetilde{f_{R}}}{\partial B}\hat{\textbf{b}}\cdot\left(\nabla\varphi\times\nabla\psi\right).

(L.4)

Upon application of the fixed-boundary adjoint relation we obtain (
5.106
) with (
5.102
).

Appendix M:   Details of effective ripple in the
1/\nu
regime calculation

Neoclassical transport in the
1/\nu
collisionality regime is discussed in many references including
[
65
]
,
[
42
]
, and
[
116
]
. In this Appendix we sketch the computation of
\epsilon_{\text{eff}}^{3/2}
originally introduced in
[
168
]
and compute linear perturbations of
f_{\epsilon}
(
5.112
), showing them to take the form of (
5.113
).

In the
1/\nu
regime, the distribution function is ordered in the parameter
\nu_{*}=\nu/(v_{t}/L)\ll 1
, where
\nu
is the collision frequency, the thermal speed is
v_{t}=\sqrt{2T/m}
for mass
m
and temperature
T
, and
L
is a macroscopic scale length,

\displaystyle f_{1}=f_{1}^{-1}+f_{1}^{0}+\mathcal{O}(\nu_{*}).

(M.1)

In velocity space we use a pitch angle coordinate

\lambda=v_{\perp}^{2}/(v^{2}B)
, energy coordinate
\epsilon=v^{2}/2
, and
\sigma=\text{sign}(v_{||})
, where
v_{\perp}=\sqrt{v^{2}-v_{||}^{2}}
is the perpendicular velocity and
v_{||}=\textbf{v}\cdot\hat{\textbf{b}}
is the parallel velocity. We use the field line label,
\alpha
, and length along a field line,
l
, to describe location on a constant
\psi
surface. In the
1/\nu
regime the
\textbf{E}\times\textbf{B}
precession frequency is assumed to be small relative to the collision frequency, so the drift kinetic equation (
4.2
) becomes,

\displaystyle v_{||}\frac{\partial f_{1}}{\partial l}=C(f_{1})-\textbf{v}_{\text{m}}\cdot\nabla\psi\frac{\partial f_{0}}{\partial\psi},

(M.2)

where the Maxwellian with density
n
is,

\displaystyle f_{0}=n\pi^{-3/2}v_{t}^{-3}e^{-v^{2}/v_{t}^{2}},

(M.3)

and the radial magnetic drift is,

\displaystyle\textbf{v}_{\text{m}}\cdot\nabla\psi=(v^{2}+v_{||}^{2})\frac{m}{2qB^{3}}\nabla\psi\times\textbf{B}\cdot\nabla B,

(M.4)

for charge
q
. The drift kinetic equation to
\mathcal{O}(\nu_{*}^{-1})
is,

\displaystyle v_{||}\frac{\partial f_{1}^{-1}}{\partial l}=0.

(M.5)

In the trapped portion of phase space, this implies that
f_{1}^{-1}=f_{1}^{-1}(\psi,\alpha,\epsilon,\lambda)
, and in the passing portion of phase space, this implies that
f_{1}^{-1}=f_{1}^{-1}(\psi,\epsilon,\lambda,\sigma)
. The drift kinetic equation to
\mathcal{O}(\nu_{*}^{0})
is,

\displaystyle v_{||}\frac{\partial f_{1}^{0}}{\partial l}=C(f_{1}^{-1})-\textbf{v}_{\text{m}}\cdot\nabla\psi\frac{\partial f_{0}}{\partial\psi}.

(M.6)

In the passing region, this implies that
f_{1}^{-1}
is a Maxwellian, so it can be taken to vanish. We employ a pitch-angle scattering operator,

\displaystyle C=\frac{2\nu(\epsilon)v_{||}}{B\epsilon}\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda}\left(\lambda v_{||}\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda}\right).

(M.7)

The parallel streaming term in (
M.6
) is annihilated by the bounce averaging operation,

\displaystyle 0=\langle C(f_{1}^{-1})\rangle_{b}-\langle\textbf{v}_{\text{m}}\cdot\nabla\psi\rangle_{b}\frac{\partial f_{0}}{\partial\psi},

(M.8)

where the bounce average of a quantity
A
is
\langle A\rangle_{b}=\tau^{-1}\oint dl\,A/v_{||}
and the bounce time is
\tau=\oint dl\,v_{||}^{-1}.
The bounce-averaged equation (
M.8
) can be expressed in terms of the parallel adiabatic invariant
J=\oint dl\,v_{||}
using the relation,

\displaystyle\langle\textbf{v}_{\text{m}}\cdot\nabla\psi\rangle_{b}=\frac{m}{q\tau}\frac{\partial J}{\partial\alpha}.

(M.9)

Integrating (
M.8
) with respect to
\lambda
we obtain,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial f_{1}^{-1}}{\partial\lambda}=\frac{m\epsilon}{2q\lambda\nu(\epsilon)}\frac{\partial f_{0}}{\partial\psi}\left(\oint dl\,\frac{v_{||}}{B}\right)^{-1}\int_{1/B_{\max}}^{\lambda}d\lambda^{\prime}\,\frac{\partial J}{\partial\alpha}.

(M.10)

Here
B_{\max}
is the maximum value of the field strength on the surface labeled by
\psi
.
We have used the boundary condition
\left(\oint dl\,v_{||}/B\right)\partial f_{1}^{-1}/\partial\lambda\rvert_{\lambda=1/B_{\max}}=0
, as there is no flux in pitch-angle from the passing region. The integration with respect to
\lambda
is performed to obtain,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial f_{1}^{-1}}{\partial\lambda}=-\frac{m}{6q\lambda\nu(\epsilon)}\frac{\partial f_{0}}{\partial\psi}\left(\oint dl\,\frac{v_{||}}{B}\right)^{-1}\frac{\partial}{\partial\alpha}\left(\oint dl\,\frac{v_{||}^{3}}{B}\right).

(M.11)

The particle flux from
f_{1}^{-1}
is obtained by multiplying (
M.6
) by
f_{1}^{-1}(\partial f_{0}/\partial\psi)^{-1}
, integrating over velocity space, and flux surface averaging,

\displaystyle\left\langle\bm{\Gamma}\cdot\nabla\psi\right\rangle_{\psi}

\displaystyle\equiv\left\langle\int d^{3}v\,f_{1}^{-1}\textbf{v}_{\text{m}}\cdot\nabla\psi\right\rangle_{\psi}=\left\langle\int d^{3}v\,f_{1}^{-1}C(f_{1}^{-1})\left(\frac{\partial f_{0}}{\partial\psi}\right)^{-1}\right\rangle_{\psi}.

(M.12)

The velocity space integration is performed using the velocity-space Jacobian
d^{3}v=2\pi\sum_{\sigma}B\epsilon/|v_{||}|d\lambda d\epsilon
. Upon integration by parts in
\lambda
and applying (
M.11
), the following expression is obtained,

\left\langle\bm{\Gamma}\cdot\nabla\psi\right\rangle_{\psi}=\\
-\frac{4\sqrt{2}\pi}{V^{\prime}(\psi)}\left(\frac{m}{3q}\right)^{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}d\epsilon\,\left(\frac{\partial f_{0}}{\partial\psi}\right)\frac{\epsilon^{5/2}}{\nu(\epsilon)}\int_{1/B_{\max}}^{1/B_{\min}}\frac{d\lambda}{\lambda}\,\int_{0}^{2\pi}d\alpha\,\sum_{i}\frac{(\frac{\partial}{\partial\alpha}\hat{K}_{i}(\alpha,\lambda))^{2}}{\hat{I}_{i}(\alpha,\lambda)},

(M.13)

where the bounce integrals are defined by (
5.111
). The sum in (
M.13
) is taken over trapping regions for particles with pitch angle
\lambda
on a field line labeled by
\alpha
for left bounce points
\varphi_{-,i}\in[0,2\pi)
.

The parameter
\epsilon_{\text{eff}}^{3/2}
quantifies the geometric dependence of the
1/\nu
particle flux.
It is defined in terms of the radial particle flux in the following way
[
168
]
,

\displaystyle\langle\bm{\Gamma}\cdot\nabla\psi\rangle_{\psi}=-32\langle|\nabla\psi|\rangle_{\psi}^{2}\left(\frac{m}{3q}\right)^{2}\frac{1}{B_{0}^{2}R^{2}}\epsilon_{\text{eff}}^{3/2}\int_{0}^{\infty}d\epsilon\,\left(\frac{\partial f_{0}}{\partial\psi}\right)\frac{\epsilon^{5/2}}{\nu(\epsilon)}.

(M.14)

We take our normalizing length and field values to be such that
B_{0}R=\epsilon_{\text{ref}}^{-1}\langle|\nabla\psi|\rangle_{\psi}
, where
\epsilon_{\text{ref}}
is a reference aspect ratio. Comparing (
M.13
) with (
M.14
) we obtain the expression for
\epsilon_{\text{eff}}^{3/2}
(
5.110
). The corresponding expression (29) in
[
168
]
is obtained by noting that
\hat{H}^{\text{Nemov}}=-(\partial\hat{K}/\partial\alpha)\lambda^{1/2}B_{0}^{3/2}
and
\hat{I}=2\hat{I}^{\text{Nemov}}
, where
\hat{H}^{\text{Nemov}}
and
\hat{I}^{\text{Nemov}}
are given in (30)-(31) of
[
168
]
.

The shape derivative of
f_{\epsilon}
(
5.112
) is computed to be,

\displaystyle\delta f_{\epsilon}(S_{P};\bm{\xi}_{1})=\int_{V_{P}}d\psi\,w(\psi)\delta(V^{\prime}(\psi)\epsilon_{\text{eff}}^{3/2}(\psi)).

(M.15)

The perturbation to the bounce integrals is computed using the following identity for the perturbation of a line integral
Q_{L}=\int_{l_{0}}^{l_{L}}dl\,Q
due to displacement of the integration curve by vector field
\delta\textbf{x}

[
9
,
138
]
,

\displaystyle\delta Q_{L}=\int_{l_{0}}^{l_{L}}dl\,\left(\delta\textbf{x}\cdot\left(-\bm{\kappa}Q+\left(\textbf{I}-\hat{\textbf{t}}\hat{\textbf{t}}\right)\cdot\nabla Q\right)+\delta Q\right)+Q(l_{L})\delta l_{L}-Q(l_{0})\delta l_{0},

(M.16)

where
\delta Q
is the perturbation to the integrand at fixed position,
\hat{\textbf{t}}=\textbf{x}^{\prime}(l)
is the unit tangent vector,
\bm{\kappa}=\textbf{x}^{\prime\prime}(l)
is the curvature, and
\delta l_{L}
and
\delta l_{0}
are perturbations to the bounds of the integral.

We compute the perturbation to the bounce integrals to be,

\displaystyle\delta\hat{I}_{i}

\displaystyle=\oint dl\,\left(-\frac{v_{||}}{vB}\bm{\kappa}\cdot\delta\textbf{x}-\left(\frac{\lambda v}{2Bv_{||}}+\frac{v_{||}}{B^{2}v}\right)\left(\delta\textbf{x}\cdot\nabla B+\delta B\right)\right)

(M.17a)

\displaystyle\delta\hat{K}_{i}

\displaystyle=\oint dl\,\left(-\frac{v_{||}^{3}}{v^{3}B}\bm{\kappa}\cdot\delta\textbf{x}-\left(\frac{3\lambda v_{||}}{2Bv}+\frac{v_{||}^{3}}{B^{2}v^{3}}\right)\left(\delta\textbf{x}\cdot\nabla B+\delta B\right)\right),

(M.17b)

where
\delta B
is the perturbation to the field strength (
5.62
) and
\delta\textbf{x}
is given by (
5.22
). We note that
\delta\textbf{x}\cdot\hat{\textbf{b}}=0
such that the perpendicular projection,
(\textbf{I}-\hat{\textbf{t}}\hat{\textbf{t}})
, is not needed. There is no contribution due to the perturbation of the bounce points, as the integrand vanishes at these points. The expressions (
5.113
)-(
5.115b
) can now be obtained by writing (
M.15
) in terms of the perturbations of the bounce integrals, using
\bm{\xi}_{1}\cdot\nabla B+\delta B=-B\left(\textbf{I}-\hat{\textbf{b}}\hat{\textbf{b}}\right)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\nabla\bm{\xi}_{1}-\delta\chi_{1}^{\prime}(\psi)\hat{\textbf{b}}\cdot(\nabla\psi\times\nabla\varphi)
and

\bm{\kappa}\cdot\bm{\xi}_{1}=-\hat{\textbf{b}}\hat{\textbf{b}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\nabla\bm{\xi}_{1}
.

Appendix N:   Details of departure from quasi-symmetry calculation

In this Appendix we compute the shape derivative of
f_{QS}
(
5.121
) to obtain (
5.126
)-(
5.127c
) by expressing each term in (
5.125
) in the desired form. The second term in (
5.125
) is expressed using
\delta\psi=-\bm{\xi}_{1}\cdot\nabla\psi
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{V_{P}}d^{3}x\,w^{\prime}(\psi)\delta\psi\mathcal{M}^{2}=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{V_{P}}d^{3}x\,\mathcal{M}^{2}\bm{\xi}_{1}\cdot\nabla w(\psi).

(N.1)

The third term in (
5.125
) is computed upon application of (
5.20
), the divergence theorem, and noting that
\mathcal{M}=\textbf{B}\cdot\bm{\mathcal{A}}
,

\int_{V_{P}}d^{3}x\,w(\psi)\mathcal{M}\delta\textbf{B}\cdot\bm{\mathcal{A}}=-\int_{S_{P}}d^{2}x\,\bm{\xi}_{1}\cdot\textbf{{n}}w(\psi)\mathcal{M}^{2}-\int_{V_{P}}d^{3}x\,w(\psi)\delta\chi^{\prime}_{1}(\psi)\mathcal{M}\nabla\psi\times\nabla\varphi\cdot\bm{\mathcal{A}}\\
+\int_{V_{P}}d^{3}x\,\bm{\xi}_{1}\cdot\left(w(\psi)\mathcal{M}\left(\textbf{B}\times\left(\nabla\times\bm{\mathcal{A}}\right)\right)-\bm{\mathcal{A}}w(\psi)\textbf{B}\cdot\nabla\mathcal{M}+\mathcal{M}\nabla\left(w(\psi)\mathcal{M}\right)\right).

(N.2)

The quantity
\bm{\mathcal{A}}
can be projected into the perpendicular direction as
\bm{\xi}_{1}\cdot\hat{\textbf{b}}=0
, noting that,

\displaystyle\hat{\textbf{b}}\times\left(\bm{\mathcal{A}}\times\hat{\textbf{b}}\right)=-(\hat{\textbf{b}}\times\nabla\psi)\nabla_{||}B-F(\psi)\nabla_{\perp}B.

(N.3)

Similarly, any terms in (
N.2
) involving
\bm{\xi}_{1}\cdot\nabla
can be expressed as
\bm{\xi}_{1}\cdot\nabla_{\perp}
. The corresponding terms in (
5.127a
) are obtained using the expression for the curvature in an equilibrium field. The fourth term in (
5.125
) is expressed in the following way upon application of (
5.62
), the divergence theorem, and noting that
\bm{\mathcal{S}}\cdot\nabla\psi=\nabla\cdot\bm{\mathcal{S}}=0
,

\int_{V_{P}}d^{3}x\,w(\psi)\mathcal{M}\bm{\mathcal{S}}\cdot\nabla\delta B=\int_{S_{P}}d^{2}x\,\bm{\xi}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{n}Bw(\psi)\bm{\mathcal{S}}\cdot\nabla\mathcal{M}-\int_{V_{P}}d^{3}x\,\bm{\xi}_{1}\cdot\left[B\nabla\left(w(\psi)\bm{\mathcal{S}}\cdot\nabla\mathcal{M}\right)\right]\\
+\int_{V_{P}}d^{3}x\,w(\psi)(\bm{\mathcal{S}}\cdot\nabla\mathcal{M})\left(\delta\chi^{\prime}_{1}(\psi)\hat{\textbf{b}}\cdot(\nabla\psi\times\nabla\varphi)+B\bm{\xi}_{1}\cdot\bm{\kappa}\right).

(N.4)

We express terms involving
\bm{\xi}_{1}\cdot\nabla
as
\bm{\xi}_{1}\cdot\nabla_{\perp}
to obtain the corresponding terms in (
5.127a
). The fifth term in (
5.125
) is expressed in the following way upon application of
\delta\psi=-\bm{\xi}_{1}\cdot\nabla\psi
, the divergence theorem, and several vector identities,

\int_{V_{P}}d^{3}x\,w(\psi)\mathcal{M}\textbf{B}\times\nabla\delta\psi\cdot\nabla B=-\int_{S_{P}}d^{2}x\,\bm{\xi}_{1}\cdot\hat{\textbf{n}}w(\psi)\mathcal{M}\nabla B\times\textbf{B}\cdot\nabla\psi\\
-\int_{V_{P}}d^{3}x\,\bm{\xi}_{1}\cdot\nabla\psi\nabla B\cdot\nabla\times\left(w(\psi)\mathcal{M}\textbf{B}\right).

(N.5)

The sixth term in (
5.125
) upon application of (
5.124
) is,

-\int_{V_{P}}d^{3}x\,\frac{\delta G(\psi)w(\psi)\mathcal{M}\textbf{B}\cdot\nabla B}{\iota(\psi)-(N/M)}=\\
\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\int_{S_{P}}d^{2}x\,\frac{w(\psi)V^{\prime}(\psi)\langle\mathcal{M}\textbf{B}\cdot\nabla B\rangle_{\psi}}{(\iota(\psi)-(N/M))}\left(\textbf{B}\cdot\nabla\psi\times\nabla\vartheta\right)\bm{\xi}_{1}\cdot\hat{\textbf{n}}\\
-\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\int_{V_{P}}d^{3}x\,\bm{\xi}_{1}\cdot\nabla\left(\frac{w(\psi)V^{\prime}(\psi)\langle\mathcal{M}\textbf{B}\cdot\nabla B\rangle_{\psi}}{(\iota(\psi)-(N/M))}\right)\textbf{B}\cdot\nabla\psi\times\nabla\vartheta\\
+\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\int_{V_{P}}d^{3}x\,\frac{w(\psi)V^{\prime}(\psi)\langle\mathcal{M}\textbf{B}\cdot\nabla B\rangle_{\psi}}{\iota(\psi)-(N/M)}\left(\bm{\xi}_{1}\cdot\left(\nabla\psi\nabla\cdot\left(\textbf{B}\times\nabla\vartheta\right)-\textbf{B}\times\nabla\times\left(\nabla\psi\times\nabla\vartheta\right)\right)\right)\\
-\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\int_{V_{P}}d^{3}x\,\delta\chi^{\prime}_{1}(\psi)\frac{w(\psi)V^{\prime}(\psi)\langle\mathcal{M}\textbf{B}\cdot\nabla B\rangle_{\psi}}{\sqrt{g}^{2}(\iota(\psi)-(N/M))}\frac{\partial\textbf{x}}{\partial\varphi}\cdot\frac{\partial\textbf{x}}{\partial\vartheta}.

(N.6)

In obtaining the corresponding terms in (
5.127a
), terms involving
\bm{\xi}_{1}\cdot\nabla
are expressed as
\bm{\xi}_{1}\cdot\nabla_{\perp}
.
The seventh term in (
5.125
) is expressed using
\delta\psi=-\bm{\xi}_{1}\cdot\nabla\psi
. Combining all terms, we obtain (
5.126
)-(
5.127c
).

Appendix O:   Details of neoclassical figures of merit calculation

In this Section we compute the shape derivative of
f_{NC}
(
5.130
) to obtain (
5.136
)-(
5.137c
) by expressing each term in (
5.135
) in the desired form. Throughout Boozer coordinates will be assumed.

The second term in (
5.135
) is expressed using
\delta\psi=-\bm{\xi}_{1}\cdot\nabla\psi
. The third term in (
5.135
) can be computed using (
5.124
), noting that
V^{\prime}(\psi)/(4\pi^{2}\sqrt{g})=B^{2}/\langle B^{2}\rangle_{\psi}
in Boozer coordinates and applying the divergence theorem,

\int_{V_{P}}d^{3}x\,w(\psi)\frac{\partial\mathcal{R}(\psi)}{\partial G(\psi)}\delta G(\psi)=-\int_{V_{P}}d^{3}x\,w(\psi)\frac{B^{2}\sqrt{g}}{\langle B^{2}\rangle_{\psi}}\frac{\partial\mathcal{R}(\psi)}{\partial G(\psi)}\bm{\xi}_{1}\cdot\nabla\psi(\nabla\times\textbf{B})\cdot\nabla\vartheta\\
+\int_{V_{P}}d^{3}x\,\left(\bm{\xi}_{1}\cdot\nabla\left(\frac{\partial\mathcal{R}(\psi)}{\partial G(\psi)}\frac{w(\psi)}{\langle B^{2}\rangle_{\psi}}\right)B^{2}G(\psi)+\frac{w(\psi)}{\langle B^{2}\rangle_{\psi}}\frac{\partial\mathcal{R}(\psi)}{\partial G(\psi)}\bm{\xi}_{1}\cdot\textbf{B}\times\nabla\times\left(\frac{\partial\textbf{x}}{\partial\varphi}B^{2}\right)\right)\\
+\int_{V_{P}}d^{3}x\,\frac{w(\psi)\delta\chi_{1}^{\prime}(\psi)B^{2}}{\sqrt{g}\langle B^{2}\rangle_{\psi}}\frac{\partial\mathcal{R}(\psi)}{\partial G(\psi)}\frac{\partial\textbf{x}}{\partial\varphi}\cdot\frac{\partial\textbf{x}}{\partial\vartheta}-\int_{S_{P}}d^{2}x\,w(\psi)\frac{B^{2}}{\langle B^{2}\rangle_{\psi}}\frac{\partial\mathcal{R}(\psi)}{\partial G(\psi)}G(\psi)\bm{\xi}_{1}\cdot\hat{\textbf{n}}.

(O.1)

The fifth term in (
5.135
) can be computed using (
5.62
), the divergence theorem, and the expression for the curvature in an equilibrium field (
5.105
),

\int_{V_{P}}d^{3}x\,w(\psi)\langle S_{\mathcal{R}}\delta B\rangle_{\psi}=\int_{V_{P}}d^{3}x\,\left(\bm{\xi}_{1}\cdot\nabla\left(w(\psi)S_{\mathcal{R}}\right)B-BS_{\mathcal{R}}w(\psi)\bm{\xi}_{1}\cdot\bm{\kappa}\right)\\
-\int_{V_{P}}d^{3}x\,\delta\chi_{1}^{\prime}(\psi)S_{\mathcal{R}}w(\psi)\hat{\textbf{b}}\cdot\nabla\psi\times\nabla\varphi-\int_{S_{P}}d^{2}x\,w(\psi)S_{\mathcal{R}}B\bm{\xi}_{1}\cdot\hat{\textbf{n}}.

(O.2)

The resulting terms can be combined to write the shape derivative in the form of (
5.136
), noting that any terms involving
\bm{\xi}_{1}\cdot\nabla
can be expressed as
\bm{\xi}_{1}\cdot\nabla_{\perp}
.

Appendix P:   Linearized equilibrium energy functional and coefficient matrices

P.1
Further simplification of energy functional

We will now further simplify the energy functional (
6.11
) using a magnetic coordinate system. Each of the contravariant components of the perturbed magnetic field are evaluated to be,

\displaystyle Q^{\psi}

\displaystyle\equiv\delta\textbf{B}[\bm{\xi}]\cdot\nabla\psi=\frac{1}{\sqrt{g}}\left(\frac{\partial\xi^{\psi}}{\partial\varphi}+\iota\frac{\partial\xi^{\psi}}{\partial\vartheta}\right)

(P.1a)

\displaystyle Q^{\vartheta}

\displaystyle\equiv\delta\textbf{B}[\bm{\xi}]\cdot\nabla\vartheta=\frac{1}{\sqrt{g}}\left(\frac{\partial\xi^{\alpha}}{\partial\varphi}-\frac{\partial\xi^{\psi}\iota}{\partial\psi}\right)

(P.1b)

\displaystyle Q^{\varphi}

\displaystyle\equiv\delta\textbf{B}[\bm{\xi}]\cdot\nabla\varphi=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{g}}\left(\frac{\partial\xi^{\alpha}}{\partial\vartheta}+\frac{\partial\xi^{\psi}}{\partial\psi}\right).

(P.1c)

We also express the current density in the contravariant basis as,

\displaystyle\textbf{J}=J^{\vartheta}\frac{\partial\textbf{x}}{\partial\vartheta}+J^{\varphi}\frac{\partial\textbf{x}}{\partial\varphi}.

(P.2)

The first term in the energy functional is expressed as,

\displaystyle W_{1}

\displaystyle\equiv-\frac{1}{\mu_{0}}\int_{V_{P}}d^{3}x\,\delta\textbf{B}[\bm{\xi}]\cdot\delta\textbf{B}[\bm{\xi}]

(P.3)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\mu_{0}}\int_{V_{P}}d^{3}x\,\Bigg[\left(Q^{\psi}\right)^{2}g_{\psi\psi}+\left(Q^{\vartheta}\right)^{2}g_{\vartheta\vartheta}+\left(Q^{\varphi}\right)^{2}g_{\varphi\varphi}+2Q^{\psi}Q^{\vartheta}g_{\psi\vartheta}\Bigg],

where
g_{x_{i}x_{j}}=\partial\textbf{x}/\partial x_{i}\cdot\partial\textbf{x}/\partial x_{j}
are the metric coefficients. Here we have assumed that
\varphi=\phi
, the geometric toroidal angle, such that
g_{\vartheta\varphi}=g_{\psi\varphi}=0
.

The second term in the energy functional is expressed as,

\displaystyle W_{2}

\displaystyle\equiv\int_{V_{P}}d^{3}x\,\bm{\xi}\cdot\textbf{J}\times\delta\textbf{B}[\bm{\xi}]

(P.4)

\displaystyle=\int_{V_{P}}d^{3}x\,\sqrt{g}\left(\xi^{\psi}\left(J^{\vartheta}Q^{\varphi}-J^{\varphi}Q^{\vartheta}\right)+Q^{\psi}\left(\xi^{\vartheta}J^{\varphi}-\xi^{\varphi}J^{\vartheta}\right)\right).

Here we can note that the radial component of MHD force balance yields
p^{\prime}(\psi)=J^{\vartheta}-\iota(\psi)J^{\varphi}
